Example 11.4 (Objective Functions on Quantitative Attributes).
• Metric-Based Utility: If v ∈V represents expected return and variance metrics, u(v) =
vreturn −λvvariance.
• Negative Cost associated with quantitative attributes: If v represents factor exposures,
u(v) = −Cost(v), where cost might relate to turnover or taxes implied by achieving those
exposures.
Note: If the primary objective (like fees) depends directly on the spoke portfolio y ∈P2 rather
than its metric gB(y), the setup can be adapted using a composite objective in the optimization
below, but here we focus on u : V →R.
11.2
Optimal Re-implementation via Constrained Optimization
The goal is, for a given hub portfolio x ∈P1, to find a spoke portfolio y ∈P2 that best achieves two
potentially conflicting aims:
1. Match the hub metric: gB(y) should be “close” to gA(x).
2. Optimize the objective: u(gB(y)) should be maximized.
We combine these into a single optimization problem. For a fixed x ∈P1, we seek y ∈P2 that
solves:
min
y∈P2
 ∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥p
V −λu(gB(y))

56


## Page 57


Here, p ≥1 (typically p = 2 for squared Euclidean distance) and λ ≥0 is a weighting parameter
balancing metric matching (minimizing distance) against objective maximization (maximizing u,
hence minimizing −u).
Let F(x, y) be the objective function depending on parameter λ:
F(x, y) = ∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥p
V −λu(gB(y))
The set of optimal solutions for a given x ∈P1 is:
y∗(x) = arg min
y∈P2
F(x, y)
We use Berge’s Maximum Theorem [10] (applied to minimization) to establish the conditions
under which this optimization problem constructs a valid horizontal morphism f : K1 →K2 in HS.
Theorem 11.5 (Berge’s Theorem for Minimizers (informal)). Let X, Y be topological spaces. Let
F : X × Y →R be a continuous function. Let C ⊆Y be a non-empty compact set. Define the
correspondence Φ : X ⇒C by Φ(x) = arg miny∈C F(x, y). Then:
1. The value function v(x) = miny∈C F(x, y) is continuous on X.
2. The argmin correspondence Φ(x) is upper hemicontinuous and compact-valued on X.
3. If, for each x ∈X, the function y 7→F(x, y) has a unique minimum over C, then Φ(x) is
single-valued and the resulting function f(x) = Φ(x) is continuous on X.
Theorem 11.6. Assume:
• The permissible sets P1 ⊆∆n and P2 ⊆∆m are closed (hence compact).
• The attribute maps gA : P1 →V , gB : P2 →V are continuous.
• The objective function u : V →R is continuous.
• The combined objective F(x, y) = ∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥p
V −λu(gB(y)) (for fixed p ≥1, λ ≥0) has
a unique minimum y∗(x) over P2 for each x ∈P1.
Then:
1. The map f : P1 →P2 defined by f(x) = y∗(x) = arg miny∈P2 F(x, y) is continuous.
2. Let K1 = P1. K1 is a valid object in HS.
3. Let K2 = f(K1) ⊆P2. K2 is compact (hence closed) and is a valid object in HS.
4. The map f : K1 →K2 constitutes a valid horizontal morphism in HS.
Sufficient conditions for uniqueness include: P2 is convex, gB is affine, the function v 7→∥v∥p
is strictly convex (which requires both p > 1 and that ∥· ∥V is a strictly convex norm, e.g., the
Euclidean norm or any ℓq norm with 1 < q < ∞), and the function v 7→−u(v) is strictly convex
(i.e., u is strictly concave).

Proof. (1) The function F(x, y) is continuous on P1×P2 because gA, gB, u, the norm, and exponenti-
ation ((·)p) are continuous. The constraint set P2 is compact (as a closed subset of ∆m) and indepen-
dent of x. Since F(x, y) has a unique minimum for each x by assumption, part 3 of Berge’s Theorem
applies directly (with X = P1, C = P2), showing that the function f(x) = arg miny∈P2 F(x, y) is
continuous. (2) K1 = P1 is closed by assumption, hence a valid object. (3) K2 = f(K1) is the
continuous image of the compact set K1. Therefore, K2 is compact. Compact subsets of Hausdorff
spaces (like ∆m) are closed. Thus K2 is a valid object. (4) K1 and K2 are valid objects, and
f : K1 →K2 is a continuous map. This matches the definition of a horizontal morphism in HS.
We restate this more simply:
Theorem 11.7 (Construction of HS Morphisms via Optimization). Assume P1 ⊆∆n and P2 ⊆∆m
are closed (hence compact), gA : P1 →V , gB : P2 →V are continuous, and u : V →R is continuous.
If gB is affine and injective, P2 is convex, 1 < p < ∞, ∥· ∥V is a strictly convex norm (e.g., the
Euclidean norm or any ℓq norm with 1 < q < ∞; this ensures v 7→∥v∥p
V is strictly convex), and
v 7→−u(v) is strictly convex, then for each x ∈P1, the minimization problem has a unique solution
y∗(x), and the map f(x) = y∗(x) is continuous.
Remark 11.8 (Role of Injectivity). The injectivity hypothesis on gB ensures that the objective
F(x, y) = ∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥p
V −u(gB(y)) is strictly convex in y, not merely convex. If gB is not
injective, distinct portfolios y ̸= y′ with gB(y) = gB(y′) would yield identical objective values,
destroying uniqueness. In practice, uniqueness can also be recovered by adding a regulariser (e.g.,
a small ∥y∥2 term) or by restricting P2 so that gB|P2 is injective.
Remark 11.9 (Significance). This theorem shows that, under standard conditions ensuring a unique
optimal solution (for example, strict convexity/concavity), the process of finding an ‘optimal’ re-
implementation that balances metric matching and objective optimization naturally constructs a
continuous map f between compact feasible spaces K1, K2. The hub space K1 is simply the initial
permissible space P1, and the spoke space K2 is the set of achievable optimal portfolios.
This
guarantees the resulting re-implementation f is automatically proper and fits coherently into the
HS double categorical structure.
Non-uniqueness
In cases where the objective is flat (e.g., non-unique solutions to linear programs) or absent (pure
constraint satisfaction), we must rely on Michael’s Selection Theorem to guarantee the existence of
a continuous re-implementation.
58


## Page 59


Theorem 11.10 (Michael’s Selection Theorem [50]). Let X be a paracompact space (e.g., a metric
space) and Y be a Banach space. Let Φ : X ⇒Y be a lower hemicontinuous correspondence with
non-empty, closed, and convex values. Then there exists a continuous function f : X →Y such that
f(x) ∈Φ(x) for all x ∈X.
Corollary 11.11 (Existence of Continuous Re-implementation). Let permissible spaces K1 and K2
be convex, closed subsets of simplices. Let R ⊆K1 × K2 be a closed alignment relation such that for
every x ∈K1, the feasible set FR(x) is convex and non-empty. If the correspondence x 7→FR(x) is
lower hemicontinuous, then there exists a continuous horizontal morphism f : K1 →K2 in HS such
that Graph(f) ⊆R. (Since K2 is a closed convex subset of Rm, Michael’s theorem guarantees a
continuous selection into Rm with values in the convex images of the correspondence, so the selection
lands in K2.)
Remark 11.12. The convexity requirement is naturally satisfied by linear constraints (e.g., budget
sets, sector limits).
The lower hemicontinuity condition intuitively requires that the “menu” of
options does not abruptly collapse in the interior of the hub space. When measurability rather than
continuity suffices, the Kuratowski–Ryll-Nardzewski measurable selection theorem [39] provides
an alternative under weaker hypotheses. This theorem ensures that the “Design” phase (defining
the menu of allowable spoke portfolios, cf.
Section 17) is mathematically compatible with the
“Execution” phase (selecting a continuous map f).
11.3
Optimal Re-implementation via Alignment Relation Constraint
An alternative scenario arises when the primary goal is to optimize an objective function u : K2 →R
on the spoke space, subject to a pre-defined alignment relation R ⊆K1 ×K2 acting as a constraint,
as in Section 5.2. Here, K1 ⊆∆n and K2 ⊆∆m are the given compact (closed) permissible portfolio
spaces, and R is a closed relation (a vertical morphism).
For a given hub portfolio x ∈K1, the set of feasible spoke portfolios is determined by the relation
R:
FR(x) = {y ∈K2 | (x, y) ∈R} ⊆K2
This set FR(x) represents all portfolios in K2 that are considered aligned with x according to R.
The optimization problem is then to select the best portfolio from this set:
max
y∈FR(x) u(y)
Let ΦR(x) be the set of optimal solutions:
ΦR(x) = arg max
y∈FR(x) u(y) ⊆K2
If optimal solutions are unique, ΦR defines a function ϕ : K1 →K2.
We again use Berge’s Maximum Theorem to determine when this process yields a valid horizontal
morphism in HS.
Theorem 11.13 (Construction via Alignment Constraint). Assume:
1. K1 ⊆∆n and K2 ⊆∆m are permissible portfolio spaces, i.e. closed (hence compact) subsets.
2. R ⊆K1 × K2 is a vertical morphism, i.e. a closed relation.
3. u : K2 →R is a continuous objective function.

FR(x) := {y ∈K2 | (x, y) ∈R}.
Assume that FR(x) is nonempty for all x ∈K1.
5. The correspondence FR : K1 ⇒K2 is upper hemicontinuous with compact values. (Note: by
Remark 11.14 below, this condition is automatic when R is a closed relation between compact
Hausdorff spaces—which is guaranteed by assumptions (1) and (2). It is listed here for logical
clarity, but in practice need not be verified separately.)
6. For every x ∈K1, the maximization problem
max
y∈FR(x) u(y)
has a unique solution.
Then:

is continuous.

Remark 11.14 (Sufficient conditions for upper hemicontinuity). A convenient sufficient condition
for the correspondence
FR : K1 ⇒K2,
FR(x) := {y ∈K2 | (x, y) ∈R},
to be upper hemicontinuous with compact values is that the alignment relation
R ⊆K1 × K2
be closed, with K1 and K2 compact Hausdorff spaces.
Indeed, if R is closed in K1 × K2, then for each x ∈K1 the fiber
FR(x) = {y ∈K2 | (x, y) ∈R}
is compact. Moreover, the closedness of R implies that the correspondence FR has a closed graph.
For correspondences with compact values into a Hausdorff space, the closed–graph property is
equivalent to upper hemicontinuity.
In practical portfolio-construction settings, relations R are typically defined by systems of in-
equalities of the form
gj(x, y) ≤0,
j = 1, . . . , m,
where each gj : K1 × K2 →R is continuous. Such relations are automatically closed, and hence
induce upper hemicontinuous feasible-set correspondences.
Consequently, the continuity of the
optimizer constructed in Theorem 11.13 follows from standard topological facts, without requiring
any interiority or convexity assumptions beyond those ensuring uniqueness of the optimum.

compact, and the intersection is compact. The continuous projection of a compact set is compact,
so FR(x) is compact.
The upper hemicontinuity of FR follows from R being a closed relation

Remark 11.15 (Significance). This theorem shows that optimization subject to alignment relation
constraints also naturally constructs valid HS objects and morphisms, provided the relation R and

This reinforces the suitability of the HS framework for modeling practical portfolio construction

Remark 11.16 (Categorical interpretation of the feasible set). Recall from Section 5.2 that a valid

the feasible set FR(x) is precisely the fiber of the relation R at x:
FR(x) = {y ∈K2 | (x, y) ∈R}.
Therefore, the optimization problem is structurally equivalent to finding a continuous selection

11.4
Coherence of Squares Constructed from Alignment Constraints
We now analyse the coherence of a square of horizontal morphisms where, instead of being con-
structed from metric-based optimization (Theorem 11.6), they are constructed via alignment re-
lation constraints as defined in Theorem 11.13.
Assume we are given four objects (K1, K2, K3, K4) and a “square” of four vertical morphisms
(alignment relations) that serve as constraints:
• Rf ⊆K1 × K2
• Rg ⊆K1 × K3
• Rg′ ⊆K2 × K4
• Rf′ ⊆K3 × K4
We also assume given continuous objective functions u2 : K2 →R, u3 : K3 →R, and u4 : K4 →R.
The horizontal morphisms f, g, f ′, g′ are then constructed (per Theorem 11.13) as the unique
optimizers over the feasible sets defined by these relations. For example, for a given x ∈dom(f),
f(x) is the unique portfolio y that maximizes u2(y) subject to the constraint that (x, y) ∈Rf.

Analysis of Commutativity
In this framework, the square does not commute in general.
The construction is inherently
path-dependent. Let’s trace the two paths for a given x ∈K1:
• Path 1 (f′ ◦g):
1. z = g(x) = arg maxz′∈FRg (x) u3(z′). This z is the “best” portfolio in K3 (according to u3)
that is aligned with x (via Rg).
2. w = f′(z) = arg maxw′∈FRf′ (z) u4(w′). This w is the “best” portfolio in K4 (according to
u4) that is aligned with z (via Rf′).
• Path 2 (g′ ◦f):
1. y = f(x) = arg maxy′∈FRf (x) u2(y′). This y is the “best” portfolio in K2 (according to
u2) that is aligned with x (via Rf).
2. w′ = g′(y) = arg maxw′′∈FRg′ (y) u4(w′′). This w′ is the “best” portfolio in K4 (according
to u4) that is aligned with y (via Rg′).
Commutativity (w = w′) fails because the intermediate “greedy” optimizations are different.
The portfolio z is chosen using Rg and u3, while y is chosen using Rf and u2. Since y and z are
generally different, the inputs to the second-stage optimizations are different, leading to different
final portfolios (w ̸= w′).
We can obtain commutativity by imposing some rather special conditions on the optimization
problems defining the square.
Theorem 11.17 (Commutativity via Bellman Consistency). Consider alignment relations Rf ⊆
K1 × K2, Rg ⊆K1 × K3, Rg′ ⊆K2 × K4, Rf′ ⊆K3 × K4 forming a commuting square of relations:
Rf′ ◦Rg = Rg′ ◦Rf (i.e., the set of final portfolios reachable via both paths is the same).
Let u4 : K4 →R be a continuous, strictly concave objective function. Define the intermediate
objective functions u2 : K2 →R and u3 : K3 →R by the Bellman value condition:
u2(y) = max{u4(w) | (y, w) ∈Rg′}
u3(z) = max{u4(w) | (z, w) ∈Rf′}
Construct the horizontal morphisms f, g, f′, g′ as the unique optimizers subject to alignment con-
straints:

Remark 11.18 (Convexity of Wx). The convexity hypothesis on Wx is a mild condition when the
alignment relations Rg, Rf′ are defined by affine constraints (linear equalities and inequalities on
portfolio weights), since the image of a convex set under composition of affine-constrained relations
remains convex. It may fail when the relations encode non-convex constraints (e.g., cardinality
bounds or discrete sector assignments).
Remark 11.19 (Set-valued intermediate optimizers). When the intermediate value functions u2 and
u3 are not strictly concave, the argmax correspondences f and g may be set-valued. The com-
mutativity conclusion—that both paths reach the same terminal portfolio w∗—remains valid re-
gardless: it depends only on uniqueness of the final optimum, not of the intermediate ones. If

Unfortunately, this theorem is rarely useful in practice, since it involves replacing the original
objective functions with the intermediate objective functions defined by the Bellman value condition.
11.5
Summary
It is convenient to summarize the results of this section as follows.
Theorem 11.20 (Coherent construction of re-implementation pipelines). Let K1, K2, K3, K4 be
permissible portfolio spaces, i.e. closed (hence compact) subsets of simplices.
Let
Rf ⊆K1 × K2,
Rg ⊆K1 × K3,
Rg′ ⊆K2 × K4,
Rf′ ⊆K3 × K4
be closed alignment relations forming a commuting square:
Rf′ ◦Rg = Rg′ ◦Rf.
Assume continuous objective functions
u2 : K2 →R,
u3 : K3 →R,
u4 : K4 →R.
(A) Existence and continuity.
Assume that for each h ∈{f, g, f ′, g′} and each admissible
input portfolio, the feasible correspondence defined by the relation Rh is nonempty, compact, upper
hemicontinuous, and admits a unique optimizer with respect to the relevant objective function.
Then the induced maps
f : K1 →K2,
g : K1 →K3,
g′ : K2 →K4,
f′ : K3 →K4
are continuous horizontal morphisms in HS whose graphs lie in the corresponding relations.
(B) General (path-dependent) behaviour.
In general, the resulting two-stage re-implementations
f′ ◦g
and
g′ ◦f
need not coincide.
Thus, even when the alignment relations commute, pipelines constructed via
greedy stagewise optimization are generically path-dependent.
(C) Bellman-consistent (path-independent) case.
Suppose in addition that the final objective
u4 : K4 →R is strictly concave, and define intermediate value functions by
u2(y) := max{u4(w) | (y, w) ∈Rg′},
u3(z) := max{u4(w) | (z, w) ∈Rf′}.
Assume the set of reachable final portfolios Wx is convex for each x, so that strict concavity of
u4 on Wx guarantees these maxima are attained uniquely (Theorem 11.17). Construct all four maps
f, g, g′, f′ as unique optimizers relative to these value functions.
Then the resulting square of horizontal morphisms commutes:
f′ ◦g = g′ ◦f.
(D) Interpretation.
Part (B) reflects genuine economic path dependence of greedy local opti-
mizations. Part (C) identifies a dynamic-programming regime in which re-implementation pipelines
become coherent: each stage optimizes future value, and the final portfolio is globally optimal among
all portfolios reachable via the composite alignment relation.
64


## Page 65


12
Propagation of Portfolio Changes and New Requirements
12.1
Propagation of Portfolio Changes
A fundamental task in portfolio management is determining how changes in a “hub” portfolio should
flow through to its “spokes.” The HS double category provides rigorous support for this process.
The horizontal morphism f : K1 →K2 acts as the propagation mechanism. A portfolio change
x ⇝x′ at the hub K1 is coherently transformed into the change y ⇝y′ at the spoke K2, where
y = f(x) and y′ = f(x′).
Alignment is modeled by a closed relation R ⊆K1 × K3.
• Role of Properness: Because f is automatically proper (Prop. 7.3), the pushforward f!R is
a closed relation (Thm. 7.4). This ensures that the re-implemented portfolio y′ cannot be a
“phantom portfolio”—a limit point that appears to satisfy the propagated alignment but has
no compliant pre-image.
• Role of Adjunction: The equivalence R ⊆f ∗S ⇐⇒f!R ⊆S (Thm. 7.7) allows alignment
to be checked in either the hub space K1 or the spoke space K2.
• Compositional Laws: Beck–Chevalley (Thm. 8.4) and Frobenius (Thm. 9.1) ensure path
independence and that filtering commutes with re-implementation.
The coherence of HS means that a change in the Hub portfolio (x ⇝x′) that respects the
alignment relation R will produce a change in the Spoke portfolio (y ⇝y′) that respects the
propagated closed alignment relation f!R.
Remark 12.1 (Diagnostic versus prescriptive use of pullback). The pullback f ∗S admits two distinct
modes of use—a distinction that is mathematical in origin but operational in consequence.
In
diagnostic mode, we ask: “given a proposed change to the hub, will the spoke’s alignment break?”
Here we compute f∗S and check whether the new hub portfolio lies in the appropriate fiber; the
pullback serves as a feasibility test. In prescriptive mode, we reverse the question: “how should we
redesign the hub so that a desired spoke alignment can be achieved?” Here f ∗S characterizes the
set of hub portfolios compatible with the spoke constraint, and we optimize within it.
A subtler case—one the basic framework handles but does not emphasize—arises when spoke
constraints are systematic: regulatory position limits, say, that apply uniformly to all client ac-
counts. Here the spokes collectively constrain the hub. The intersection T
i f∗
i Si over all spoke
constraints defines the feasible region for the hub, and the direction of prescription reverses.
12.2
Propagation of New Alignment Requirements
New alignment or compliance requirements can be imposed on the hub and must be propagated
downstream to the spokes—possibly through several stages. In practice, portfolio changes are more
often triggered by new alignment requirements than by changes in investment conviction.
To illustrate the practical necessity of the topological and categorical results established in
previous sections, consider a multi-stage investment pipeline. In this scenario, a centralized “Hub”
strategy is transformed first into an intermediate “Model” portfolio, and subsequently into hundreds
of customized “Client” accounts.
65


## Page 66


The Scenario: New CIO Asset Allocation Guidance
A Chief Investment Officer (CIO) has determined that portfolios must now conform to centralized
asset allocation guidance. This investment framework is formalized as follows: the asset alloca-
tion guidance corresponds to a portfolio z ∈KAsset Classes, and “acceptable conformance” of actual
portfolios to guidance corresponds to an alignment relation
RCIO : KHub 99K KAsset Classes
The ongoing portfolio management problem is as follows: whenever the CIO asset allocation
guidance changes, the hub portfolio may need to be changed in order to conform to the new guidance.
Changes then need to be propagated to the spoke portfolios, and this may be a multi-stage process.
That is, the propagation of this guidance may involve a composite re-implementation process:
KHub
f−→KModel
g−→KClient
New constraints may be introduced at each stage of this pipeline:
1. Stage 1 (Hub →Model): The map f re-implements the Hub strategy into a Model portfolio.
At this stage, a Sector Constraint SSec is applied (e.g., strictly aligning with maximum sector
weights).
2. Stage 2 (Model →Client): The map g re-implements the Model into final Client ac-
counts. At this stage, Tax and Fee Constraints TTax are applied (e.g., limiting turnover for
tax efficiency).
The question is whether the final client portfolio z ∈KClient—which has been subjected to
sector constraints SSec and tax constraints TTax—actually satisfies the original CIO guidance RCIO.
The double category HS ensures that it does, and that the audit trail remains unbroken across this
multi-stage construction:
• Conformance via Properness (Theorem 7.4): Because the re-implementation maps f
and g are proper, the pushforward of the CIO’s guidance moves securely through the chain.
The set of valid client portfolios derived from the CIO’s guidance, (g ◦f)!RCIO, is guaranteed
to be a closed set. This ensures that the intersection with local constraints at each stage
is well-defined; no “phantom” client portfolios can be generated that technically satisfy the
solver tolerances but violate the fundamental CIO directive.
• Audit Conservatism via Lax Beck–Chevalley (Proposition 8.1): While strict path
independence requires specific conditions (pointwise cartesianness), the lax Beck–Chevalley
condition holds in full generality:
f′
!(g∗RCIO) ⊆h∗(f!RCIO)
In operational terms, this inclusion acts as a safety guarantee for hub-side verification. It
asserts that if we verify conformance early (filtering the hub portfolios against the CIO guid-
ance before re-implementation), the resulting client portfolios are mathematically guaranteed
to satisfy the downstream conformance checks. While some conforming portfolios might the-
oretically be found via “late verification” that cannot be traced back to the hub (unless the
square is pointwise cartesian), the lax condition ensures that the upstream audit is strictly
conservative and never produces false positives.

• Coherent Filtering via Frobenius (Theorem 9.1): The Frobenius law ensures that sector
compliance can be checked at either the hub or the model level without affecting the outcome.
Applying Frobenius to f : KHub →KModel (with RCIO on KHub and SSec on KModel):
f!(RCIO ∩f∗SSec) = f!(RCIO) ∩SSec
The LHS filters at the hub (pulling the sector constraint back via f ∗before re-implementation);
the RHS filters at the model (intersecting after re-implementation). Pushing both sides for-

 f!(RCIO ∩f∗SSec)

∩TTax = g!



In business terms, the set of permissible client portfolios is identical regardless of whether we
incorporate sector compliance at the hub level (via the pullback f ∗SSec, before Stage 1) or at
the model level (via SSec, between Stages 1 and 2). The CIO’s guidance is not “diluted” or
lost as it passes through the subsequent layers of constraints.

## Page 68


13
Computational Considerations
13.1
Representing Objects and Morphisms
For computational work in HS:
• Objects K: Represented as polyhedral sets K = {x ∈∆n | Ax ≤b}. These are intersections
of closed half-spaces, so they are always closed (and thus valid objects).
• Vertical Morphisms R: Represented as R = {(x, y) ∈K1 × K2 | Cx + Dy ≤e}. These are
also closed.
• Horizontal Morphisms f:
– If f(x) = Fx + g is affine, it is continuous.
– If f(x) is the solution to the optimization problem in Section 11, it is continuous (by
Thm. 11.6).
13.2
Computing Pullback
Given f : K1 →K2 and S = {(y, z) ∈K2 × K3 | Q(y, z) ≤0}:
f∗S = {(x, z) ∈K1 × K3 | Q(f(x), z) ≤0}
If f is affine (f(x) = Fx + g) and S is polyhedral (Py + Qz ≤q):
f∗S = {(x, z) ∈K1 × K3 | P(Fx + g) + Qz ≤q}
This is a simple matrix-vector operation.
13.3
Computing Pushforward
Given f : K1 →K2 and R = {(x, z) ∈K1 × K3 | C(x, z) ≤0}:
f!R = {(y, z) ∈K2 × K3 | ∃x ∈K1 : y = f(x), C(x, z) ≤0}
This is a projection operation. If f and C are linear, this is Fourier-Motzkin elimination [70], which
has high computational complexity.
In general, however, computing the pushforward is even more difficult because it requires pro-
jecting a nonlinear set
{(x, y, z) | y = f(x), (x, z) ∈R}
onto the (y, z) coordinates. This is a nonlinear projection or quantifier-elimination problem, which
is intractable in general.
Fortunately, in a high dimensional production setting, we rarely need to visualize or delineate
the boundary of f!R precisely. We only need to answer: “Does this specific spoke portfolio y satisfy
(y, z) ∈f!R ? In other words, does there exist x ∈K1 such that f(x) = y and (x, z) ∈R ?” Even for
N = 500, if f is defined by convex optimization and R is convex, this verification step is a standard
convex feasibility problem, which is tractable for modern solvers.
There are, however, several special cases where delineating f!R is tractable in practice.
68


## Page 69


1. Smooth and Locally Invertible Maps
If f is smooth and locally invertible (Jacobian non-singular), one can invert the relation locally:
f!R = {(f(x), z) | (x, z) ∈R}.
In such cases, the pushforward can be computed directly by evaluating f pointwise on samples of
R, making it practical for low-dimensional applications.
2. Piecewise Linear or Affine Maps
When f is piecewise affine (common in optimization-based mappings that switch regimes) and R is
convex or polyhedral, f!R is a finite union of convex projections:
f!R =
[
i
(AiRi),
where each region Ri corresponds to one affine piece of f.
Each projection is computationally
tractable using linear programming or convex elimination.
3. Low-Dimensional Nonlinear Maps
If the domain of f is low-dimensional (e.g., two to five assets) and R is defined by linear or quadratic
constraints, the pushforward can be approximated numerically by discretizing x, evaluating y =
f(x), and taking the convex hull of the resulting (y, z) pairs.
This method provides accurate
permissible-region estimates for practical portfolio problems.
4. Linear or Convex Codomain Constraints
If one wishes to verify inclusion f!R ⊆C and C is convex (for instance, linear alignment constraints),
the check reduces to
∀(x, z) ∈R,
f(x) ∈Cz,
Cz = {y | (y, z) ∈C}.
When f is continuous and Cz is convex, this inclusion can be verified via convex relaxation or
interval enclosures of f, such as McCormick envelopes [47] or semidefinite relaxations.
5. Linearization via Tangent Cones
When R describes local alignment conditions, the pushforward can be approximated by the action
of the Jacobian of f on tangent cones:
T(f(x),z)(f!R) = Df(x) T(x,z)R.
This linearized pushforward is analytically tractable and corresponds to standard sensitivity analysis
or first-order propagation in differentiable portfolio transformations.
Financial Interpretation.
In portfolio settings, R typically encodes linear alignment constraints
(e.g., exposure matching), while f represents a nonlinear optimization-based re-implementation
(e.g., fee or tax optimization). When f is continuous and proper, pushforwards can be reliably
delineated through sampling or convexification. However, certain realistic cases, such as discrete
constraints arising from lot size requirements, may not be handled well.

max
y∈∆m UB(y)
subject to
∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥V ≤ϵ
(and y ∈∆m, which is polyhedral). This is often a Linear Program (LP), Quadratic Program (QP),
or Second-Order Cone Program (SOCP), all of which are efficiently solvable [15, 11].
13.5
A Concrete Worked Example
To make the pullback and pushforward operations concrete, consider a simple system:
• Ambient Spaces: Let the hub be ∆2 = {(x0, x1, x2) ∈R3 | P xi = 1, xi ≥0} (3 assets) and
the spoke be ∆1 = {(y0, y1) ∈R2 | y0 + y1 = 1, yi ≥0} (2 assets).
• Object K1: A permissible space with a cap on asset 0:
K1 = {x ∈∆2 | x0 ≤0.5}
• Object K2 and K3: Let K2 = K3 = ∆1.
• Horizontal Morphism f : K1 →K2: An aggregation map that combines assets 0 and 1:
f(x0, x1, x2) = (x0 + x1, x2) = (y0, y1)
This is continuous and maps K1 to K2 (since x0 + x1 + x2 = 1 =⇒y0 + y1 = 1).
Computing the Pullback f∗S (Pre-alignment)
Let’s define a spoke-space alignment S ⊆
K2 × K3 that limits the aggregated asset y0 relative to some benchmark z0:
S = {(y, z) ∈∆1 × ∆1 | y0 ≤2z0}
By Definition 6.1, the pullback f ∗S finds all hub portfolios x ∈K1 that satisfy this alignment after
re-implementation:
f∗S = {(x, z) ∈K1 × K3 | (f(x), z) ∈S}
= {(x, z) ∈K1 × ∆1 | ((x0 + x1, x2), z) ∈S}
= {(x, z) ∈K1 × ∆1 | (x0 + x1) ≤2z0}
The resulting computation is simple, yielding a new closed relation on the hub space.
Computing the Pushforward f!R (Forward Propagation)
Now, define a hub-space align-
ment R ⊆K1 × K3 that constrains asset 0 against the benchmark z0:
R = {(x, z) ∈K1 × ∆1 | x0 ≥z0}
By Definition 6.4, the pushforward f!R finds all spoke-space pairs (y, z) that can be “hit” by an
aligned hub portfolio x ∈R:
f!R = {(y, z) ∈K2 × K3 | ∃x ∈K1 : f(x) = y and (x, z) ∈R}
To compute this, we must eliminate x subject to the constraints:
70


## Page 71


1. x ∈K1 =⇒x0 ≤0.5 (and x ∈∆2)
2. f(x) = y =⇒x0 + x1 = y0 and x2 = y1
3. (x, z) ∈R =⇒x0 ≥z0
4. xi ≥0 =⇒x0 ≥0, x1 ≥0, x2 ≥0
From (2) and (4), x1 = y0 −x0 ≥0 =⇒x0 ≤y0. We need to find if there exists an x0 that satisfies
all conditions on x0:
x0 ≥0
and
x0 ≤0.5
and
x0 ≥z0
and
x0 ≤y0
Such an x0 exists if and only if the “floor” is less than or equal to the “ceiling”:
max(0, z0) ≤min(0.5, y0)
Thus, the pushforward (the result of the projection) is:
f!R = {(y, z) ∈∆1 × ∆1 | max(0, z0) ≤min(0.5, y0)}
As guaranteed by Theorem 7.4, this is a closed set, but its definition is not completely trivial and
emerges from the quantifier elimination.

73


## Page 74


14
An Axiomatic Framework
The double category HS constructed in Part I is a thin equipment (framed bicategory) in the sense of
Shulman [62] and Grandis–Paré [32]: companions are graphs of maps, conjoints are their converses,
and the adjunction f! ⊣f∗follows from the unit/counit inequalities. This is standard categorical
machinery, and the axioms are stated below for completeness.
The interest of the axiomatic treatment lies elsewhere — in identifying what fails when the
framework is extended to the probabilistic setting. In HSP-r (Part III), horizontal composition of
stochastic kernels introduces error accumulation: the composite pushforward (g ◦f)! is contained
in, but generally strictly smaller than, the staged pushforward g!(f!). The companion construction,
which requires strict functoriality, cannot be carried out. The pushforward and pullback operations
become primitive predicate transformers rather than compositions with a distinguished vertical
arrow. Section 14.5 develops this observation into a “Lax Logical Framework” that identifies exactly
which axioms survive (adjunction, sub-functoriality, lax Beck–Chevalley, lax Frobenius) and which
do not (companions, conjoints, strict functoriality).
This is the section’s contribution: not the
axioms themselves, but the precise diagnosis of where they break.
Guide to this section.
Readers familiar with equipments may skim Sections 14.1–14.4 and
proceed directly to Section 14.5. Readers whose primary interest is the concrete models may skip
this section entirely; the variant semantics in Sections 15–17 and Part III do not depend on the
abstract axioms.
14.1
Axioms
Notation. We write ∧for the binary meet in the vertical poset; in the concrete model HS, this is
set-theoretic intersection ∩.

(V2) Vertical composition is monotone and distributes over meets when one factor is a companion
or conjoint: for any vertical morphisms R, R′ and any companion ⟨f⟩or conjoint [f],
⟨f⟩◦(R ∧R′) = (⟨f⟩◦R) ∧(⟨f⟩◦R′),

(V3) A dagger (converse) (−)† : Dv(A, B) →Dv(B, A) with (R†)† = R and (S ◦R)† = R† ◦S†.
(V4) The modular law (Dedekind’s formula): for composable S and R,
(S ◦R) ∧T ≤S ◦(R ∧(S† ◦T)).
In HS, axiom V4 is a standard identity of the calculus of relations: if (y, z) ∈(S ◦R) ∩T, the
witness x with (y, x) ∈R and (x, z) ∈S also satisfies (y, z) ∈T; since (y, z) ∈T and (x, z) ∈S
(equivalently (z, x) ∈S†), we get (y, x) ∈S† ◦T, hence (y, x) ∈R ∩(S† ◦T), placing (y, z) in
S ◦(R ∩(S† ◦T)). The modular law holds in every model of this paper whose vertical morphisms
are relations (including HSP-r and the three probabilistic compliance frameworks), but it is only used
in the deterministic equipment setting where companions exist (Theorem 14.10). The probabilistic
models lack companions and use only the forward Frobenius inclusion, which does not require V4.
On allegories as an abstraction of categories of relations, see [24]; for the logical foundation of
adjoint quantifiers (f! ⊣f∗), see [41]. For restriction categories, see [19].
Remark 14.2 (Why V2 is restricted to companions and conjoints). In HS, meet-distribution holds
when one factor is a function graph: Graph(f) ◦(R ∩R′) = (Graph(f) ◦R) ∩(Graph(f) ◦R′) and
(R ∩R′) ◦Graph(f) = (R ◦Graph(f)) ∩(R′ ◦Graph(f)), because a function graph assigns a unique
image to each element. The unrestricted version (distribution for arbitrary vertical composites)
fails for the same reason it fails in Rel: the standard counterexample (R = {(a, b)}, R′ = {(a, c)},
T = {(b, d), (c, d)}, giving T ◦(R ∩R′) = ∅⊊{(a, d)} = (T ◦R) ∩(T ◦R′)) applies to finite compact
spaces with closed relations.

For example, in HS the companion of a horizontal morphism f is the graph Graph(f) and the
conjoint is the reverse graph Graph(f)†, related by E3.

14.5
Lax Semantics and the Failure of Companions
The axioms of a standard equipment (Definition 14.3) imply strict functoriality for the pushforward
and pullback operations: (g◦f)! = g!◦f!. However, as seen in the probabilistic extensions (Section 18,
specifically Theorems 20.6 and 20.5), practical risk management often obeys only lax functoriality,
where composition leads to an accumulation of error bounds or risk budgets.
We therefore outline a generalized framework where the rigorous equality of the equipment is
relaxed to an inequality.
Definition 14.12 (Lax Logical Framework). A Lax Logical Framework consists of a double
category structure where:
1. The horizontal category Dh and vertical posets Dv are defined as in Definition 14.1.
77


## Page 78


2. For every horizontal morphism f : A →B, there exist monotonic mappings f! : Dv(A, ·) →
Dv(B, ·) and f∗: Dv(B, ·) →Dv(A, ·).
3. Adjunction: f! ⊣f∗holds as a Galois connection: f!R ≤S ⇐⇒R ≤f∗S.
4. Unitality: (idA)!R = R and (idA)∗S = S for all R, S.
5. Lax Composition (Sub-functoriality):
(g ◦f)!R ≤g!(f!R)
and
f∗(g∗S) ≤(g ◦f)∗S
Note: these two inequalities are mates under the adjunction in item (3) and are therefore
equivalent.
Pushforward ⇒pullback. Suppose (g ◦f)!R ≤g!(f!R) for all R. We wish to show f ∗(g∗S) ≤
(g ◦f)∗S for all S. From (g ◦f)!R ≤g!(f!R), apply (g ◦f)∗to both sides and use the unit
R ≤(g ◦f)∗((g ◦f)!R) to obtain R ≤(g ◦f)∗(g!(f!R)). By the adjunction g! ⊣g∗, g!(f!R) ≤S
iff f!R ≤g∗S, and similarly f!R ≤g∗S iff R ≤f∗(g∗S). Chaining: whenever (g ◦f)!R ≤S,
we get R ≤f∗(g∗S); but (g ◦f)!R ≤S iff R ≤(g ◦f)∗S by item (3), so f∗(g∗S) ≤(g ◦f)∗S.
Pullback ⇒pushforward. Suppose f ∗(g∗S) ≤(g ◦f)∗S for all S. We wish to show (g ◦f)!R ≤
g!(f!R). Set S = g!(f!R). Then (g ◦f)∗S ≥f∗(g∗S) ≥f∗(f!R) ≥R, where the last step uses
the unit of f! ⊣f∗. By item (3), R ≤(g ◦f)∗S iff (g ◦f)!R ≤S = g!(f!R).
The dual framework (with reversed inequalities, modeling super-functorial error accumulation)
can be obtained by swapping f! and f∗.
Remark 14.13 (The unavailability of Companions). In a strict equipment, the pushforward is defined
constructively via the companion: f!R = ⟨f⟩◦R. This definition forces strict functoriality because
the assignment f 7→⟨f⟩is a functor from the horizontal category to the vertical bicategory.
In a Lax Logical Framework, pushforwards and pullbacks cannot be defined via com-
panions and conjoints. Proof: Suppose f!R = ⟨f⟩◦R for some vertical arrow ⟨f⟩. Then:
(g ◦f)!∆A = ⟨g ◦f⟩
and
g!(f!∆A) = ⟨g⟩◦⟨f⟩.
If the physics of the system dictates a strict inequality (e.g., (g ◦f)! < g! ◦f! due to convexity
accumulation), then ⟨g ◦f⟩̸= ⟨g⟩◦⟨f⟩. This means the mapping from horizontal maps to vertical
relations is not a functor, destroying the structural coherence required for a standard equipment.
Consequently, in lax settings such as HSP-r, the operations f! and f∗must be treated as primi-
tive predicate transformers rather than as compositions with distinguishing morphisms. We lose
the ability to represent the function f as a “perfect” relation, because in a lax system, no relation
is perfectly composable.
Remark 14.14 (Lax Beck–Chevalley: A Guiding Principle). In a Lax Logical Framework, the strict
Beck–Chevalley equality f′
!(g∗S) = h∗(f!S) of Theorem 8.4 generally fails.
However, the safe
(conservative) direction
f′

can survive under suitable hypotheses. Informally, if the laxness is sub-functorial in the pushforward
direction ((g ◦f)!R ≤g!(f!R), i.e., the composite pushforward under-approximates the staged
pushforward), and if the pullback–pushforward adjunction is preserved at each stage, then (2) holds
by the same mates argument as in the strict case, with equalities replaced by inequalities throughout.
Concrete instances of this principle, with precise hypotheses and proofs, appear in the proba-
bilistic setting:

Adjunction as the Primary Safety Guarantee
While the failure of the companion construction prevents us from treating horizontal morphisms as
“perfect” relations, the adjunction between pushforward and pullback survives. In a lax framework,
this adjunction becomes the primary definition of system safety.
Proposition 14.15 (Adjunction in a Lax Framework). Even in a Lax Logical Framework where
horizontal composition is sub-functorial (i.e., (g ◦f)! ≤g! ◦f!), the operations for a single morphism
f form a Galois connection:
f!R ≤S ⇐⇒R ≤f∗S
Remark 14.16. This persistence of adjunction is critical. As established in the probabilistic model
(Theorem 20.10), this equivalence provides the logical justification for “Stress Testing”. It guarantees
that checking if a hub portfolio x lies in the preimage f ∗S is sufficient to ensure that the resulting
spoke portfolio f(x) lands in S, even if f is noisy or imperfect.
Lax Frobenius: The Cost of Leakage
In a strict equipment (like HS), the Frobenius reciprocity law is an equality: f!(R ∧f∗S) = f!R ∧S.
This implies that filtering a portfolio for compliance before re-implementation is identical to filtering
it after.
In a Lax Logical Framework, “leakage” of risk or mass (as seen in the HSP-r model) breaks this
equality. It is replaced by a one-way inclusion that dictates the safe order of operations.
Proposition 14.17 (Lax Frobenius / Conservative Filtering). In a Lax Logical Framework, filtering
upstream is strictly more conservative than filtering downstream:
f!(R ∧f∗S) ≤f!R ∧S
Proof. Let (y, z) ∈f!(R ∧f∗S). Then there exists x with (x, z) ∈R ∧f∗S and f(x) = y. Since
(x, z) ∈R, we have (f(x), z) = (y, z) ∈f!R. Since (x, z) ∈f∗S, we have (f(x), z) = (y, z) ∈S.
Therefore (y, z) ∈f!R ∧S.
In a lax setting (e.g., HSP-r), the inclusion is strict because the probabilistic pushforward f ϵ
!
“leaks” mass: a hub portfolio satisfying both R and f ∗S with individual risk budgets may fail the
joint budget under the composite pushforward, while a spoke portfolio could “luckily” satisfy S after
the fact.
Intuition. The term f!(R∧f∗S) represents the set of spoke portfolios derived from hub portfolios
that are guaranteed to satisfy S (pre-verified). The term f!R∧S represents the set of spoke portfolios
that happen to satisfy S after the fact.
In a lax system (e.g., probabilistic), there exist “lucky” outcomes where a hub portfolio with a
10% failure risk generates a compliant spoke (accepted by the RHS), but would have been rejected
by the strict upstream check (LHS). To maintain a valid audit trail, the framework must enforce
the LHS (upstream filtering) to prevent this “tail risk leakage”.
79


## Page 80


15
Evidence of Alignment
15.1
Sketch: Extension to Non-Thin 2-Cells
The axiomatic framework presented above assumes a thin double category, where the vertical
arrows in any given hom-set Dv(A, B) form a poset. This is reflected in the main model HS, where
2-cells are simply inclusions Graph(g) ◦R ⊆S ◦Graph(f). Such a 2-cell is unique if it exists,
signifying only whether a re-implementation preserves an alignment.
This, however, is a simplification of practical financial reality. Alignment may be binary; veri-
fication of alignment almost never is. It is measured by continuous metrics—tracking error, factor
loading differences, fee budgets—and a portfolio is not just “aligned” but “aligned with 35 basis
points of tracking error.”
In this particular case, one could replace the binary alignment relation R with a family of
relations Rϵ, indexed by a tracking error tolerance ϵ.
However, such ad hoc solutions become
unsatisfactory in the case of more complex alignment relations, where it is desirable to organize
verification data more systematically.
A natural extension, therefore, is to accommodate non-thin (or “non-locally-posetal”) 2-cells.
This would allow the framework to model situations where there are multiple, distinct ways for a
re-implementation to be compatible with an alignment.
A non-thin 2-cell α could represent a specific, quantitative measure or method of this compat-
ibility. For example, consider two different optimal re-implementation strategies, α1 (minimizing
fees) and α2 (minimizing tracking error). Both might satisfy the same alignment inclusion, but they
represent different, non-equivalent processes. In a non-thin framework, α1 and α2 could be distinct
2-cells filling the same square.
To accommodate this, the axiomatic framework would be modified:
1. Drop Thinness: The assumption that the double category is thin would be removed.
2. 2-Cell Hom-Sets: For any square of 1-morphisms (f, g, R, S), the 2-cells α filling that square
would form a set (or a new poset, in a “locally posetal” model) rather than being a single
boolean fact.

3. Composition and Interchange: This change would require introducing explicit axioms
for the horizontal and vertical composition of these 2-cells. Crucially, the interchange law,
which is automatic in the thin HS model, would become a non-trivial axiom that must be
explicitly satisfied.
15.2
“Evidence” 2-Cells via the Double Category of Spans
We now sketch a concrete instance of the non-thin framework just described, which is the most
natural candidate for practical applications.
We replace the category of relations with a category of spans. We assume an ambient category
E with pullbacks (e.g., Top or CompHaus, consistent with the proper maps in the main text).

Vertical Morphisms as Spans of Evidence
In this richer framework, a vertical morphism (alignment) from permissible space A to B is not a
subset of A × B, but a span in E:
A

Interpretation:
• E is the Space of Evidence (or the space of alignment certificates).
• A point e ∈E is a specific witness that portfolio p(e) ∈A is aligned with portfolio q(e) ∈B.
• The physical alignment relation from the thin model is recovered by taking the joint image
⟨p, q⟩(E) ⊆A × B.

The Double Category Span(E)
We construct the double category Span(E) as follows:
1. Objects: Permissible portfolio spaces A, B, . . . (objects of E).
2. Horizontal Morphisms: Continuous maps f : A →B (the re-implementations). Note that
the horizontal category Dh is simply E itself.
3. Vertical Morphisms: Spans S = (A
pS
←−ES
qS
−→C).
4. 2-Cells (Evidence Transformations): A 2-cell α is a morphism of spans in the standard
sense.
Note that pullbacks in E are required to define composition of spans.
Motivation: In the thin framework, a 2-cell asserted that “if x aligns with z via S, then f(x)
aligns with g(z) via T.” In this framework, the 2-cell α acts as an evidence transformer. It takes
a certificate of alignment for the hub portfolios and systematically transforms it into a certificate of
alignment for the spoke portfolios.
This is essential for audit trails. It is not enough to know that the ETF basket is compliant; we
require a map α that derives the ETF’s alignment report directly from the Mutual Fund’s alignment
report.
82


## Page 83


Horizontal Morphisms as a Full Subcategory
To integrate standard re-implementations into the vertical structure, we use the companion con-
struction. Any horizontal map f : A →B induces a vertical span (its graph):
Graph(f) := (A
id
←−A
f−→B)
This allows us to view horizontal re-implementations as a special case of alignment (“perfect de-
terministic alignment”). The fact that horizontal morphisms embed fully into the span structure
ensures that we can treat “re-implementation” and “alignment” uniformly when needed, while re-
taining the directional distinction for the user.
Technical Trade-off: Weak Associativity
The move from relations to spans introduces a layer of algebraic complexity. While relations form
an associative algebra (sets satisfy (A∩B)∩C = A∩(B ∩C)), spans form a bicategory. Pullbacks
are associative only up to isomorphism:
(E ×B F) ×C G ∼= E ×B (F ×C G)
For practical portfolio systems, this requires handling coherence isomorphisms (bookkeeping). The
thin HS model in the main text suppresses this complexity to focus on topological properness, but
a fully automated “Evidence Engine” would likely require the span-based bicategorical structure to
manage the data structures of alignment reports.
Worked Example: Evidence Spans in a CIO Pipeline
To make the construction concrete, consider the three-stage pipeline from Section 12: a CIO model
portfolio x ∈KCIO is re-implemented into a sector model y = f(x) ∈KSec, which is then re-
implemented into a client portfolio z = g(y) ∈KClient.
In the relational (thin) setting, the alignment constraint between KCIO and KSec is a closed
relation R ⊆KCIO × KSec encoding, say, “tracking error ≤50bps.”
The 2-cell is binary: either
(x, y) ∈R or it is not.
In the span setting, we replace R with a span
KCIO
π1
←−−ER
π2
−−→KSec
where ER is the evidence space: a set of triples (x, y, c) where c is a compliance certificate
— a data structure recording the tracking error ∥gA(x) −gB(y)∥, the fee differential, the date of
computation, and the solver parameters used. The projections π1, π2 recover the hub and spoke
portfolios.
Suppose a second alignment span S represents a fee-budget constraint between KSec and KClient,
with evidence space ES recording fee computations and budget attestations. The composite span
S ◦R is formed by pullback:
ES◦R = ER ×KSec ES = {(x, y, c1, z, c2) : π2(x, y, c1) = π′
1(y, z, c2)}
An element of ES◦R is a composite audit trail: a tracking-error certificate c1 linking CIO to sector,
paired with a fee-budget certificate c2 linking sector to client, joined at the common intermediate
portfolio y. This is precisely the data structure an Evidence Ledger (Appendix D) would store. The
thin framework records only whether (x, z) satisfies the composite constraint; the span framework
records which certificates were used at each stage and through which intermediate portfolio they
compose.

15.3
Beck–Chevalley and Frobenius
In the baseline HS framework, the Beck–Chevalley and Frobenius laws were expressed as equalities
of sets (relations). In the Span(E) framework, these laws become natural isomorphisms between
spans. This shift is significant for software implementation: it means we can structurally convert
audit trails between different stages of the pipeline without information loss.
The following results are standard.

of horizontal morphisms (continuous maps) in E:

analog of the “pointwise cartesian” condition from Definition 8.2).
In the span framework, the
Beck–Chevalley condition asserts an isomorphism of evidence spaces for any vertical alignment S
on B:
f′

Operational Meaning: Suppose S represents a benchmark constraint on B.
• The LHS (f′
! g∗S) represents: “Pull the benchmark constraint back to A (pre-alignment), find
evidence of alignment there, and push that evidence forward to C.”
• The RHS (h∗f!S) represents: “Push the benchmark constraint forward to D (post-alignment),
and pull back to check if C’s re-implementation satisfies it.”
The isomorphism guarantees that an alignment certificate generated by the “early verification”
process (LHS) can be losslessly translated into an alignment certificate for the “late verification”
process (RHS). The audit trail is preserved across the lattice of re-implementations.
Frobenius Reciprocity (Preservation of Filtered Evidence).
For a horizontal morphism
f : A →B and vertical alignments R (on A) and S (on B), the Frobenius law describes the
interaction between filtering and re-implementation:
f!(R ×A f∗S) ∼= f!R ×B S
Operational Meaning:
• LHS (Filter then Re-implement): We find portfolios in A that satisfy alignment R and
whose re-implementations satisfy S. We bundle this combined evidence and push it to B.
• RHS (Re-implement then Filter): We take evidence that A satisfies R, push it to B, and
pair it with independent evidence that the result satisfies S.

## Page 85


In the relational model, this was a statement about the set of permissible portfolios remaining
constant. In the span model, the isomorphism explicitly constructs the composite witness. It tells
us that we do not need to re-run the alignment check S from scratch after re-implementation; we
can essentially “carry over” the witness from the hub analysis.

16
Persona–Indexed Hub–and–Spoke
This section develops a variant of the HS calculus in which every “object” is a family of permis-
sible portfolio spaces indexed by a base of personas. Formally, we work in the slice over a fixed
compact Hausdorff space B of personas (age bands, client segments, life–cycle states, etc.). Ob-
jects are display maps p : K →B whose fibers Kb ⊆∆n(b) are closed permissible portfolio spaces.
Horizontal morphisms are continuous, fiberwise re–implementations over B; vertical morphisms are
closed, persona–indexed alignment relations. All coherence results (closed pushforwards, Adjunc-
tion, Beck–Chevalley, Frobenius) lift fiberwise and hence globally.
The intuition is that B is a space of investor personas. For example:
• B is a range of ages, e.g.
the closed interval [25, 95].
This can be used to model target
date glide paths (see [63]), capturing the requirement that re-implementations and alignment
specifications be compatible with the way that the portfolio in a glide path evolves as the
investor ages.
• B is a product space incorporating both the age and educational attainment of the investor.
• B = [0, 1] is an interpolation parameter between two existing hub portfolios. Given K0, K1 ⊆
∆n, define Kt = (1−t)K0 +tK1 (Minkowski combination; closed in ∆n since both summands
are compact). The family K = F
t∈[0,1] Kt is a persona-indexed object, and any t ∈(0, 1)
defines a “virtual hub” interpolating between the two endpoint strategies—allowing a client to
hold a portfolio “between” two existing products without designing a new hub from scratch.
Throughout we assume B compact Hausdorff unless stated otherwise. This matches key use–cases
(closed age interval, finite demographic factors, aggregate wealth, etc.), and makes properness au-
tomatic again. In practice B will usually be a simple space.
While the main applications below involve connected B, the finite (hence discrete) case com-
monly occurs—a series of model portfolios indexed by risk level, say. Mathematically trivial; orga-
nizationally, not.
16.1
Persona–indexed Permissible Spaces
Definition 16.1 (Persona–indexed object). Fix a compact Hausdorff base B. A persona–indexed
permissible portfolio space is a continuous map p : K →B such that K is a closed subset of B ×∆n
for some n, and for each b ∈B the fiber
Kb = {x ∈∆n | (b, x) ∈K}
is a closed subset of ∆n. We often write K = F
b∈B Kb suggestively.
Interpretation. For each persona b (e.g., age b), Kb is the permissible region under baseline con-
straints for that persona. Continuity in b lets constraints (caps, budgets, liquidity) vary smoothly
along demographic axes.
Lemma 16.2 (Compactness). If B is compact Hausdorff and K ⊆B × ∆n is closed, then K is
compact and the projection p : K →B is proper.
Let pi : Ki →B and qj : Lj →B be objects over B.
Definition 16.3 (Horizontal morphisms over B). A horizontal morphism f : K1 →K2 over B is
a continuous map with p2 ◦f = p1. Equivalently, a family {fb : K1,b →K2,b}b∈B of continuous
re–implementations varying continuously in b.

by B) from K1 to L1 is a closed subset
R ⊆K1 ×B L1 = {(x, z) ∈K1 × L1 | p1(x) = q1(z)},
i.e., a closed, fiberwise relation Rb ⊆K1,b × L1,b.
Horizontal composition and vertical (relational) composition are computed over B. We write
GraphB(f) = {(x, f(x)) ∈K1 ×B K2}
for the graph of a horizontal map over B.
Proposition 16.5 (Automatic properness over a compact base). Let B be compact Hausdorff. For
any horizontal morphism f : K1 →K2 over B between objects pi : Ki →B as in §16.1, the map f
is proper. Moreover, for any object r : M →B, the product map
f ×B idM : K1 ×B M −→K2 ×B M
is proper and hence closed.

f!R = (f ×B idM)(R) ⊆K2 ×B M.
Theorem 16.7 (Closedness of pushforward over B). If B is compact Hausdorff, f : K1 →K2 is
horizontal, and R ⊆K1 ×B M is closed, then f!R is closed in K2 ×B M.
Proof sketch. By §16.1, Ki and M are compact; thus the fiber product K1 ×B M is compact and R
is compact. The map f ×B idM is continuous, hence it maps the compact R to a compact (therefore
closed) subset of K2 ×B M.

## Page 89


(2) Age × educational attainment.
B = [bmin, bmax] × E with E a finite discrete set (e.g.,
{HS, College, Graduate}). Finite E is compact; thus B is compact. Piecewise–constant features in
e ∈E and continuous in age satisfy the hypotheses; all laws apply componentwise over E.
(3) Age × wealth.
B = [bmin, bmax] × [wmin, wmax] ⊂R2 (age × wealth level). In standard
lifecycle portfolio theory (Merton [48], Samuelson [59]), the optimal asset allocation is a function of
both the investor’s time horizon (age) and current wealth—through mechanisms such as decreasing
relative risk aversion, subsistence floors, or housing considerations. Let K ⊆B × ∆n encode joint
age–wealth constraints: for instance, the equity cap may decrease with age (de-risking) while the
allocation to alternatives increases with wealth (access to illiquid vehicles requires higher minimums).
A horizontal morphism f : K →K′ over B implements a re-implementation that is continuous in
both b (age) and w (wealth); vertical alignment relations are fiberwise in K(b,w) × K′
(b,w). The
product of two compact intervals is compact Hausdorff, so all results of Section 16 apply.
(4) Non–contractible bases.
Non–contractibility does not impede the calculus; compactness
does the work. However, the practical use cases are more speculative.
• B = S1 (spending seasonality): Annual spending patterns are approximately periodic—
tuition payments in autumn, tax payments in spring, holiday expenditures in winter—so the
“time of year” parameter lives naturally on the circle S1 ∼= R/Z. A family K ⊆S1×∆n encodes
seasonal cash and duration targets (e.g., higher cash allocation in the months preceding large
outflows), with constraints that wrap continuously around the year-end boundary rather than
exhibiting a discontinuity at December/January. All coherence laws hold fiberwise over S1.
• B = S1 (investment-cycle regimes): Tactical asset allocation often depends on where
the economy sits in a recurring regime cycle. For example, in the growth–inflation clock (cf.
Greer [33]), the economy rotates through four quadrants—recovery, expansion, stagflation,
contraction—and the recommended factor tilts (value vs. growth, duration, commodities)
vary continuously around the cycle.
Modeling the regime parameter as b ∈S1 captures
the periodicity: the “post-contraction” regime borders “pre-recovery” without a boundary
discontinuity. Families K ⊆S1 × ∆n then encode regime-dependent permissible allocations,
and re-implementations (e.g., from a strategic to a tactical sleeve) are continuous in the regime
parameter.
• B a finite connected graph (a 1–dimensional CW complex): Vertices represent discrete
states—career stages (student, employed, unemployed, retired), product-lifecycle phases (launch,
growth, maturity, decline), or plan statuses (accumulation, payout, rollover)—and edges repre-
sent permissible transitions between them. Loops are allowed: an employee can cycle between
“employed” and “unemployed” multiple times, and a product can re-enter a growth phase after
repositioning. Under the standard CW topology, any finite graph is compact Hausdorff. A
display map p : K →B assigns to each state v a fiber Kv ⊆∆n of permissible allocations, and
to each edge a continuous family of intermediate allocations interpolating between the end-
point fibers (modeling a transition glide path). Coherence results (adjunction, Beck–Chevalley,
Frobenius) persist because they depend only on compactness of B, not on its homotopy type.

Portfolio reading. Aggregating persona attributes or refining them induces functorial reindexing of

16.4
Cross-Vintage Constraints
A key reason to consider persona-indexed portfolio spaces is that it is often insufficient to consider
constraints on individual portfolios. It is often the case that a “series” of portfolios is required to
satisfy cross-portfolio constraints. The HS/B framework allows us to specify this formally.
For example, for suites of target date funds indexed by retirement year r, it is often desirable
to enforce cross-vintage constraints. Consider a constraint on the steepness of the glide path: as
calendar time advances by ∆, the allocation for the r-vintage at age b differs from the allocation
for the (r + ∆)-vintage at age b + ∆by no more than ϵ.
Standard slice categories restrict vertical morphisms to the fiber product K×B L, forcing aligned
portfolios to map to the exact same base point b. This prevents us from modeling constraints that
link different personas, such as these cross-vintage constraints. To address this, we generalize the
vertical morphisms in HS/B.
Definition 16.14 (Generalized Vertical Morphism over B). Let p : K →B and q : L →B be
objects (display maps). A vertical morphism is a pair (r, R) where:
1. r ⊆B ×B is a closed relation on the base space (e.g., the “time shift” relation r = {(b, b+∆) |
b ∈[0, T −∆]}).
2. R ⊆K × L is a closed relation on the total spaces.
3. Compatibility: The relation R lies over r. That is, for every (x, y) ∈R, the pair of base
points (p(x), q(y)) must belong to r.
Example 16.15 (Formulation of Glide Path Steepness Constraint). Let x(t)
b
denote the allocation
for vintage t at age b. A smoothness constraint requires |x(t)
b
−x(t)
b+∆| ≤ϵ. This is a relation R
covering the shift relation r = {(b, b + ∆)} on the age axis B. Because R and r are closed, vertical
composition of generalized morphisms preserves closedness (by the same argument as Lemma 4.5,
applied fiberwise).
The standard coherence laws (Beck–Chevalley, Frobenius) for the slice HS/B hold when vertical
morphisms are restricted to the fiber product K ×B L. For generalized morphisms which allow
cross-fiber relations, the coherence laws require additional hypotheses on the interaction between
the base relation r and the display maps. In the glide path setting, these conditions are satisfied
when the shift map b 7→b + ∆is a proper self-map of B.
16.5
Summary
By replacing single spaces K ⊆∆n with persona–indexed display maps p : K →B and working in
the slice HS/B, we obtain a calculus in which every HS notion is fibered over personas. With B
compact Hausdorff (a natural condition for many segmentations), properness is again automatic,
pushforwards preserve closedness, and Adjunction, Beck–Chevalley, and Frobenius hold verbatim,
persona by persona.

## Page 91


This supports glidepaths, demographic conditioning, and even non–contractible persona bases
(circles/graphs) without changing the proofs—only the ambient bookkeeping. It also supports prac-
tical considerations in glide path design, in particular cross-portfolio (i.e. cross-vintage) constraints.

## Page 92


17
DOTS for Portfolio Systems
This variant framework is based on a suggestion from David Jaz Myers. The idea is simple: instead
of committing to a single spoke per hub—which is what the HS framework does when it asks for a
deterministic re-implementation f—define a menu of permissible spokes and let the portfolio man-
ager choose from it. The choice may involve qualitative judgment, client preferences, or constraints
that resist formalization; what matters is that the menu itself is well-defined, that it composes
sensibly, and that compliance can be verified at the menu level rather than at the level of individual
selections. This is the DOTS approach (see [44], [51]). This section provides only a sketch of the
theory.
The primitive operation replaces the deterministic re-implementation with a set-valued one.
Given an alignment relation R specifying a constraint on a desired re-implementation (as in Sec-
tion 5.2), we act on a “state”—a closed subset representing the current portfolio universe—and
obtain a new closed subset representing the menu of permissible outcomes:
K ⊙R := { y : ∃x ∈K, (x, y) ∈R }.
As we will see this unifies screens, budgets, and optimizers as algebraic operations; it supports
parallel assembly via a symmetric monoidal product; and it elevates strategy blueprints to operadic
operations. When unique optimizers exist, DOTS collapses to HS and all HS guarantees apply.
Otherwise, DOTS retains and exposes path dependence so that it can be managed, audited, and—
where possible—reduced.
17.1
The DOTS Equipment
The thin double category
Define DOTS with the following data:
• Objects: simplices ∆n, n ≥0.
• Horizontal arrows: continuous maps f : ∆n →∆m.
• Vertical arrows: closed relations R ⊆∆n × ∆m.
• 2-cells: inclusions Graph(g) ◦R ⊆S ◦Graph(f).
Composition is by ordinary function composition horizontally and relational composition vertically.
Thinness (only inclusions) keeps algebraic reasoning close to elementary set manipulations [62, 32]
while still supporting a full calculus of pushforwards, pullbacks, and Frobenius reciprocity.
We could also define our objects to be closed permissible subsets K ⊆∆n as before, but for
simplicity, we use the above definition for now.
States as loose morphisms and the action
A state on ∆n is a closed subset K ⊆∆n, viewed as a loose morphism ∆0 ⇒∆n. Given a closed
state K ⊆∆n and a closed relation R ⊆∆n × ∆m, the action is the pushforward
K ⊙R := { y ∈∆m : ∃x ∈K, (x, y) ∈R }.
Proposition 17.1 (Action laws). For closed K and closed relations R, S the following hold:
(a) Closedness: K ⊙R is closed.
92


## Page 93


(b) Unitality: K ⊙∆∆n = K, where ∆∆n is the identity relation on ∆n.
(c) Associativity: (K ⊙R) ⊙S = K ⊙(S ◦R), where S ◦R denotes the standard relational
composition (right-to-left: first apply R, then S).
(d) Isotonicity: K ⊆K′ implies K ⊙R ⊆K′ ⊙R.
(e) Projectors: If R = { (x, x) : x ∈E } with E closed, then K ⊙R = K ∩E and (K ⊙R) ⊙R =
K ⊙R.
Proof. Closedness: Let S := (K × ∆m) ∩R. Both K × ∆m and R are closed in ∆n × ∆m, which
is compact; hence S is compact. The projection πy : S →∆m is continuous, so πy(S) = K ⊙R is
compact and closed.
Associativity: We compute directly:
(K ⊙R) ⊙S = {z : ∃y ∈∆m, ∃x ∈K, (x, y) ∈R, (y, z) ∈S}
= {z : ∃x ∈K, (x, z) ∈S ◦R} = K ⊙(S ◦R).
Projectors: If R = {(x, x) : x ∈E} with E closed, then
K ⊙R = {x ∈∆n : ∃x′ ∈K, (x′, x) ∈R} = K ∩E.
Idempotence follows since (K ∩E) ∩E = K ∩E.
Remark 17.2 (Finance mapping). (a) Closedness means menus are stable under limits in the portfolio
space: there are no phantom spokes created by limit points. (b) Unitality expresses that “doing
nothing changes nothing.” (c) Associativity says we may act by R and then by S, or equivalently
by the composite constraint S ◦R. (d) Isotonicity states that broadening the hub can only expand
the menu. (e) Projectors model idempotent screens such as ESG, liquidity, or admissibility filters.
Remark 17.3 (Generalizing horizontal morphisms). Although the action K ⊙R is defined using
vertical composition, it serves the functional role of a generalized horizontal transition.
In the
main HS framework (Section 5.2), we sought a single deterministic re-implementation f : K →L
respecting R. Here, the object K ⊙R represents the space of all possible re-implementations—
effectively a “cloud” or “menu” of spoke portfolios permissible under the constraint R.
Thus, the DOTS action algebraizes the “feasible region” concept from Section 11.3, allowing us
to manipulate sets of potential spokes before a final selection is made.
Remark 17.4 (Deterministic slice and bridge back to HS). If R = Graph(f) for a continuous
f : ∆n →∆m, then K ⊙R = f(K), which is closed because K is compact.
Thus, whenever
an action reduces to a graph (e.g., a unique optimizer), we are back in the original HS theory and
inherit all its guarantees.
Remark 17.5 (Non-determinism and selection). In the general case where K ⊙R is not a graph,
implementing menu selection means making a continuous selection of a spoke portfolio in the menu
for each hub portfolio in K, e.g. via some optimization process. Theorem 11.10 guarantees that
this is possible in practical settings.
Remark 17.6 (DOTS vs. HSP-r). It is instructive to compare the DOTS approach with the stochas-
tic HSP-r framework (Section 18).
• HSP-r (Distributions): Models uncontrolled variance (e.g., solver noise, liquidity slippage).
The system produces a distribution, and we require that almost all outcomes are compliant.

• DOTS (Menus): Models controlled choice (e.g., human manager discretion, regime selec-
tion). The system produces a set of valid options, and we require that the selection capability
is preserved.
Ideally, a pipeline uses DOTS for the design phase—narrowing the menu of permissible strategies—
and HSP-r for the execution phase, modeling the noisy implementation of the selected strategy.
Design is about choice; execution is about noise. The two require different mathematics.
The gap between the two frameworks is, however, not as clean as this distinction suggests.
In practice, the “design” phase involves solver runs whose outputs are themselves noisy, and the
“execution” phase involves human overrides that are better modeled as menu selections than as
stochastic perturbations. The frameworks are complementary, but neither alone captures the full
pipeline—a point the operadic formalism acknowledges implicitly by treating composition abstractly,
without specifying whether its components are deterministic, set-valued, or stochastic.
Remark 17.7 (Multi-hub alignment). The operadic composition above handles building a spoke from
multiple inputs: the core–satellite wiring diagram, for instance, takes two hub spaces and produces
one output. DOTS handles this naturally. A distinct—and harder—situation arises when a single
spoke must remain aligned with multiple independent hubs simultaneously: a portfolio that must
track both a regional equity benchmark and a global fixed-income benchmark managed by separate
teams. This is a multi-objective alignment problem. The spoke’s constraint set is the intersection of
pullbacks from each hub, and feasibility of the intersection is not guaranteed. The current framework
does not claim to solve this problem in general, though it does provide the tools to formulate it
precisely: the intersection T
j f∗
j Sj is well-defined and closed (each pullback is closed, and a finite
intersection of closed sets is closed), so the question reduces to nonemptiness—a feasibility problem
the framework poses but does not resolve.
17.2
Encoding Frequent Portfolio Relations
We list canonical closed relations used repeatedly in practice and indicate how to encode them.
Throughout, the emphasis is on making each modeling choice explicit so that its mathematical
properties (closedness, convexity) are clear to downstream consumers.

17.3
Operadic Templates
In the DOTS paradigm, operadic templates encode multi-input wiring patterns. Each template
consists of an input profile, a family of internal relations, and an output interface; evaluating
the template on input states returns a menu. This formalism describes a simple framework for
modularity and reusability in portfolio construction.
95


## Page 96


Core–satellite
To illustrate the practical utility of operadic composition, we define a reusable wiring diagram Φ
for a Core-Satellite strategy. In the operadic view (see [43]), Φ is an operation with arity 2: it
accepts two permissible hub spaces (the inputs) and produces a single permissible total portfolio
space (the output).
Let the inputs be:
• Kcore ⊆∆n: A high-capacity, low-fee permissible space (e.g., an Index Fund hub).
• Ksat ⊆∆m: A high-alpha, concentrated permissible space (e.g., a Hedge Fund hub).
The wiring diagram Φ encapsulates the logic of combining these independent sleeves into a
unified strategy. It is defined by a weight parameter w ∈[0, 1] (the allocation to Core) and a global
constraint relation Rglobal. The composition is given by:
Φ(Kcore, Ksat) = (Kcore ⊗Ksat) ⊙Rmix,w ⊙Rglobal
Here, Rmix,w is the structural relation that creates the composite portfolio y = w · xc + (1 −w) · xs,
and Rglobal represents portfolio-level constraints that cannot be checked at the sleeve level, such as
a total fee cap or a portfolio-level volatility cap.
We treat Φ as a “black box” circuit that wires the two inputs together (Figure 17). The power of
the framework is that Kcore and Ksat can be swapped out for any other valid objects (e.g., replacing
the Index Fund with an ETF basket) without redesigning the compliance logic encoded in Φ.

97


## Page 98


When BC and Frobenius fail
BC can fail if the square is not pointwise cartesian (missing preimages) or if some map loses proper-
ness (objects are not closed). Frobenius fails if images are forcibly closed after the fact (post hoc
closure), because topological closure does not distribute over intersections or composition. Finan-
cially this manifests as phantom spokes and order-sensitive surprises that are hard to debug.
17.5
Case Studies: End-to-End Pipelines
Each case specifies (i) inputs, (ii) relations and their order, (iii) points of determinization, (iv)
guaranteed equalities (BC, Frobenius), and (v) computational notes. The aim is to make explicit
which steps commute and which encode genuine choices.
Multi-manager blend with fee and factor budgets
Inputs. KA ⊆∆nA, KB ⊆∆nB (closed).
Relations. Rblend ⊆(∆nA ⊕∆nB) × ∆m, fee-cap projector Rfee≤τ, factor projector Rfac≤b. Here ⊕
denotes the direct sum of simplices (juxtaposition of two independent weight vectors into a single
higher-dimensional space) and ⊗denotes the product of permissible spaces KA × KB. The menu is
K⋆= (KA ⊗KB) ⊙Rblend ⊙Rfee≤τ ⊙Rfac≤b.
Determinization. If blending solves a strictly convex program, replace Rblend by Graph(fblend);
then, by Frobenius, fee and factor projectors commute with fblend.
Computation. Rfac≤b and Rfee≤τ are typically polyhedral; Rblend is often conic (SOCP). Use elim-
ination to derive K⋆or sample-and-hull for outer approximations, retaining certificates of inclusion.
Tax-aware ETF transition with turnover gate
Inputs. Legacy state Klegacy ⊆∆n.
Relations. Rturn≤κ, Rtax≤θ, tracking Rtrack,ϵ. The pipeline is
K⋆= Klegacy ⊙Rturn≤κ ⊙Rtax≤θ ⊙Rtrack,ϵ.
Order. If Rtrack,ϵ is functional (unique optimizer), Frobenius allows moving projectors across it.
Otherwise, the order encodes a real choice: “turnover-first” versus “tax-first” yields different menus.
Practice. Apply the liquidity projector before tracking to avoid infeasible optimizers; log the exact
order of operations for audit.
Factor neutralization overlay
Inputs. Kbase ⊆∆n.
Relations. Overlay relation Rhedge targeting factor exposures and max gross notional; fee projector.
The output is
K⋆= Kbase ⊙Rhedge ⊙Rfee≤τ.
Determinization. With strictly convex penalties on residual exposure and notional, Rhedge =
Graph(fhedge). By Frobenius, fee filtering may occur before or after fhedge without changing the
menu.

Benchmark-aware customization

K⋆= Kclient ⊙Graph(b) ⊙Rtrack,ϵ ⊙RESG ⊙Rliq.
BC. If a later factor map commutes pointwise cartesianly with b, BC allows reordering checks across

18
Probabilistic Hub-and-Spoke Theory
18.1
Motivation
This variant framework, based on a suggestion of Greg Zitelli’s, extends the basic topological theory
to cover practical situations in which optimization is approximate, nondeterministic, or subject to
“measure zero” exceptions. The underlying categorical structure mimics Part I, but the mathe-
matical foundations shift significantly—from point-set topology to infinite-dimensional analysis [2],
set-valued analysis [8, 58], and measure theory [14, 12]. The probabilistic theory is, as a consequence,
considerably more technical than the basic theory. This is not merely a matter of exposition; the
complications are genuine.
From Audit to Risk Management
In the idealized HS framework, we assumed portfolio re-implementation is deterministic. In the
graded extension developed below (HSP-r, Definition 18.1), we will see that any possible outcome,
no matter how unlikely, could in principle be tracked for audit purposes.
However, production
environments often require a probabilistic perspective:
1. Stochastic Solvers: Real-world constraints may be non-convex, requiring heuristic solvers
that produce distributions of outcomes.
2. Tail Risk vs. Core Behavior: A strategy should not be invalidated by a “one-in-a-billion”
solver glitch or an extreme tail event. We require a formalism that ignores events below a
specified probability threshold (the risk budget).
3. Value-at-Risk (VaR): Compliance rules are often probabilistic (e.g., “The loss shall not
exceed L with 99% confidence”).
To model these phenomena, we extend our category to one in which horizontal morphisms are
nondeterministic, and 2-cells carry a “risk budget” parameter ϵ.
Informally, the picture is this: a deterministic re-implementation sends each hub portfolio to
exactly one spoke portfolio. A stochastic re-implementation sends each hub portfolio to a probability
distribution over spoke portfolios. The question “is the spoke aligned with the hub?” becomes “is the
spoke aligned with the hub with high enough probability?”—and the answer depends on a tolerance
parameter ϵ that quantifies how much tail risk is acceptable. The mathematical machinery below
(Polish spaces, tight Feller kernels, Chapman–Kolmogorov composition) exists to make this question
precise and to ensure that the answer composes correctly across multiple stages. The reader who
finds the machinery heavy should keep in mind that its purpose is modest: to define what “almost
always compliant” means in a way that is stable under composition.
18.2
The Graded Double Category HSP-r
We define a double category based on tight Feller kernels equipped with an ϵ-support semantics.
Unlike the strict audit framework, which tracks all possibilities, HSP-r (Hub-and-Spoke Risk)
tracks the “effective” behaviour of the system up to a defined tolerance.
Definition 18.1 (HSP-r Data). The [0, 1)-graded double category HSP-r (Hub-and-Spoke
Risk) consists of:

Composition of 2-cells. Risk budgets accumulate additively under composition:
1. Horizontal composition (stages of a pipeline): if the left square has a 2-cell at level δ and
the right square at level ϵ, the composed square has a 2-cell at level δ + ϵ (Theorem 20.5).
Concretely, the risk of a two-stage pipeline is bounded by the sum of the per-stage risks.
2. Vertical composition (stacking alignment constraints): if the top square has a 2-cell at level
δ and the bottom at level ϵ, the composed square (with vertically composed relations T ◦R
and U ◦S) has a 2-cell at level δ + ϵ.
Proof sketch. Consider two vertically composable squares sharing a middle kernel Q : Z ⇝W.
The top square has kernel P : X ⇝Y , relations R ⊆X × Z, S ⊆Y × W, and 2-cell at level
δ. The bottom square has kernel Q′ : V ⇝V ′, relations T ⊆Z × V , U ⊆W × V ′, and 2-cell
at level ϵ. The composed square has kernel P (top), kernel Q′ (bottom), relations T ◦R and
U ◦S.
Let (x, v) ∈T ◦R, witnessed by z with (x, z) ∈R and (z, v) ∈T. Introduce the auxiliary
triple product measure P(x, ·) ⊗Q(z, ·) ⊗Q′(v, ·) on Y × W × V ′.
The top 2-cell gives
PrP⊗Q[(y, w) ∈S] ≥1 −δ, and the bottom 2-cell gives PrQ⊗Q′[(w, v′) ∈U] ≥1 −ϵ. By the
union bound on the triple product:

3. Identity 2-cells: For any relation R ⊆X × Z, the identity square (with Dirac kernel δid on
top and R on both sides) admits a 2-cell at risk level ϵ = 0, since δid(x, Rz) = 1Rz(x) = 1 for
every (x, z) ∈R.
This definition embeds the deterministic theory HS as the “zero-risk” limit (ϵ = 0). Horizontal
morphisms in HS become Dirac kernels in HSP-r, i.e. deterministic kernels.
18.3
Tight Feller Kernels: Modeling Imperfect Optimization
We restrict horizontal morphisms to tight Feller kernels. This ensures that the system is stable
under perturbations and that the “bulk” of the probability mass remains within a compact region,
allowing ϵ-supports to be well-defined compact sets.
Definition 18.2 (Feller Property). A Markov kernel P(x, dy) is Feller if the map x 7→
R
ϕ(y)P(x, dy)
is continuous for every bounded continuous function ϕ.
Interpretation: Small changes in the input (hub) lead to small changes in the distribution of
outputs (spoke). This is the analog of continuity in the deterministic case.
Definition 18.3 (Tightness). A kernel P is tight if for every compact K ⊆X and δ > 0, there
exists a compact L ⊆Y such that P(x, L) ≥1 −δ for all x ∈K.
Interpretation: This guarantees that valid bounded inputs produce valid bounded outputs with
high probability. Mass does not “escape to infinity.” This is the analog of properness in the deter-
ministic case.
104


## Page 105


Example 18.4 (Failure of Tightness: Unbounded Leverage). Consider an optimization problem
where short selling is permitted without gross exposure limits. If the solver detects an arbitrage
opportunity, the solution sequence {y(k)} may diverge (wA →+∞, wB →−∞). The probability
mass describing the solution escapes. For any compact subset L, limk→∞P(x, L) = 0. This violates
tightness, confirming that unbounded solvers are not valid morphisms.
Remark 18.5. The Feller and tightness conditions are natural in the sense that they generalize ex-
actly the continuity and properness on which the deterministic theory depends. Whether they are
checkable for a given production solver is a different question. Most commercial optimizers do not
expose enough of their internal structure to verify the Feller property analytically; one can test it
empirically (perturb the input, observe the output distribution) but this is not a proof. The honest
position is that the HSP-r framework provides the correct target specification for a well-behaved
stochastic pipeline, while verification that a particular implementation meets the specification re-
mains, in most cases, an empirical matter.
