Example 18.6 (Production optimizers: Gurobi). Standard MIP solvers are discontinuous (not
Feller). To use them in HSP-r, we apply randomized smoothing by adding noise σξ to the objective
(where σ > 0 is the noise magnitude and ξ is a standard random variable, e.g. Gaussian).
P(x, dy) = Law
 argminy∈K [C(x, y) + σξ]

While this creates a kernel with potentially infinite topological support (if ξ is Gaussian), the ϵ-
support concentrates tightly around the optimal solution lattice. The HSP-r framework allows us
to ignore the Gaussian tails that extend to the boundary of the feasible region, treating the solver
as effectively “sharp” up to a confidence interval.
Example 18.7 (Production optimizers: Axioma). Similarly, a smoothed Axioma kernel Pσ is
tight because the long-only budget constraint is compact. While the strict topological support is
the entire simplex (due to Gaussian smoothing of alpha signals), the ϵ-support correctly identifies
the “likely” portfolios.
105


## Page 106


18.4
Probabilistic Operations: Two Approaches
To define the operations of pullback and pushforward in a probabilistic setting, we must identify
a geometric object that captures the “bulk” of the probability mass. We explore two approaches:
concentration of measure and highest density region. Before developing the results, we introduce
and compare these two approaches.
The first approach (suggested by Greg Zitelli) models the safety region as a metric ball centered
on the solver’s expected output. This approach relies on foundational concentration inequalities.
This approach works well for production solvers (e.g. Gurobi, Axioma) in cases where there is a
global optimum and the error is typically sub-Gaussian.
Definition 18.8 (Centered Kernel and Safety Radius). Let P : X ⇝Y be a tight Feller kernel
between metric spaces (X, dX) and (Y, dY ). We assume P is a perturbation of a deterministic center
map f : X →Y (e.g., the noise-free solution). For a risk budget ϵ > 0, the Safety Radius rϵ is a
scalar satisfying:
P(x, B(f(x), rϵ)) ≥1 −ϵ
for all x ∈X
where B(c, r) = {y ∈Y | dY (c, y) ≤r} denotes the closed metric ball.
Remark 18.9 (Derivation via Concentration Inequalities). If the solver error is bounded or sub-
Gaussian, rϵ can be derived analytically. For example, using Hoeffding’s inequality for bounded
perturbations [36], or Talagrand’s concentration inequality for product spaces [66], one typically
obtains a radius scaling as rϵ ∝
p
ln(1/ϵ). This replaces the complex density integration of the
HDR approach with a single global parameter.
The second approach tracks the exact density contours.
Definition 18.10 (ϵ-Highest Density Region). Let Y be a Polish space and P(x, ·) a probability
measure on Y with a continuous density function ρx. The ϵ-Highest Density Region (ϵ-HDR),
denoted suppϵ(P(x)), is the super-level set of the density containing probability mass 1 −ϵ:
suppϵ(P(x)) := {y ∈Y | ρx(y) ≥λϵ}
where λϵ = sup{λ ≥0 | P(x, {y | ρx(y) ≥λ}) ≥1 −ϵ}.
Remark 18.11. If the density ρx has no flat regions (i.e., the measure of level sets {y | ρx(y) = c} is
zero for all c), then λϵ is the unique threshold satisfying the mass condition with equality.
Remark 18.12 (Stability of Production Solvers). For the Gaussian-smoothed Gurobi example
(Example 18.6), the addition of Gaussian noise σξ typically induces a quasi-concave (unimodal)
density around the optimal lattice points. This ensures that the ϵ-HDR is a finite union of compact
convex sets (or a single convex set in the relaxed case) that evolves continuously with the input x.
Similarly, for Axioma, signal smoothing ensures the alpha landscape is sufficiently regular that the
HDR is stable. Unlike the intersection definition, the HDR remains robust for both discrete and
continuous error models.
Computational Tractability.
In the HDR approach, calculating the density-based ϵ-support of
a distribution defined by a solver (e.g., a convex optimization problem with random perturbations)
requires numerical integration or Monte Carlo sampling to delineate the boundary of the high-
density region. This is O(ed) in dimension d. In contrast, the Safety Radius approach requires
only the calculation of the Euclidean distance d(f(x), Sc
z). For polyhedral constraints (common in
finance), this is a standard convex projection problem, solvable in polynomial time.
106


## Page 107


Architecture Impact.
For the Safety Radius approach, in the system architecture described in
Appendix D, the Verification Worker can now be stateless and purely geometric. It does not need to
simulate thousands of solver runs to estimate a failure probability, as it does for the HDR approach.
Instead, it queries the Solver Interface for the deterministic solution f(x) and the pre-calibrated
Lipschitz constant/variance parameters to determine rϵ. It then performs a geometric containment
check against the constraints stored in the Morphism Registry.

Table 2: Comparison of the Safety Radius and Highest Density Region (HDR) approaches
The Safety Radius approach is generally preferred in a portfolio replication context. Here are
some examples where portfolio construction can generate irregular probability distributions (multi-
modal or “kidney-shaped”), forcing the use of the HDR approach.
Minimum Position Sizes (The “Split Peak”)
This is the most common cause of multimodal
distributions in institutional systems.
• The Constraint: A rule requiring that if an asset is held, it must constitute at least a certain
percentage of the portfolio (e.g., “Hold 0% or ≥2%”). This is common to avoid “dust” positions
that are costly to trade.
• The Scenario: An optimizer wants to allocate an ideal weight of 1.0% to a stock.
• The Resulting Distribution:
– Because 1.0% is illegal, a randomized solver (or a solver with input noise) will “dither.”
– In 50% of runs, it rounds down to 0%.
– In 50% of runs, it forces up to 2%.
• Why Metric Concentration Fails:
– The “average” or “center” of this distribution is 1%.
– A metric ball centered at 1% suggests that 1% is a valid, safe holding.
– In reality, 1% is explicitly forbidden.
The Highest Density Region (HDR) correctly
identifies two separate islands of safety (0 and 2+), while the metric ball includes the
“valley of death” between them.
107


## Page 108


Tax-Aware Transitioning (The “Kidney” or “Banana”)
This scenario creates non-convex,
curved distributions, often referred to as banana-shaped or kidney-shaped.
• The Constraint: A combination of a maximum tracking error (an ellipsoid constraint) and a
maximum realized capital gain tax budget (a linear or piecewise-linear constraint).
• The Scenario: You are transitioning a legacy portfolio to a new target. You want to minimize
distance to the target, but you have a strict limit on how much tax you can generate.
• The Resulting Distribution:
– The solver tries to sell assets to buy the new target.
– It hits the tax cap. To get closer to the target without selling high-cost basis stock, it
starts buying “proxy” assets (highly correlated substitutes) that don’t trigger taxes.
– As correlation shifts, the efficient frontier of solutions curves around the tax obstacle.
• Why Metric Concentration Fails:
– The set of valid solutions curves around the “expensive” tax event.
– A metric ball centered on the mean would cut through the tax constraint violation region.
– The HDR correctly wraps around the obstacle in a kidney shape, excluding the high-tax
center.
Regime Switching Signals (The “Bifurcation”)
This generates highly separated multimodal
distributions.
• The Constraint: A quantitative signal that acts as a binary switch. For example, “If Volatility
< 15, leverage is 150%; if Volatility > 15, leverage is 100%.”
• The Scenario: Current market volatility is measuring exactly at 15.0, but there is measurement
noise (±0.1).
• The Resulting Distribution:
– Small perturbations in the input data cause the solver to flip aggressively between two
distinct portfolios (High Leverage vs. Low Leverage).
– The output distribution has two dense clouds of solutions that are far apart in Euclidean
space.
• Why Metric Concentration Fails:
– The mean of these two clouds is a portfolio with 125% leverage.
– However, the strategy never allows 125% leverage.
– A metric ball would declare the 125% leverage portfolio “safe,” creating a false positive
for audit.
108


## Page 109


Long/Short Gross Exposure Limits (The “Hollow Shell”)
This creates distributions where
the “center” is empty.
• The Constraint: A fund must maintain exactly 0% net exposure (dollar neutral) but maximize
exposure to a specific factor, subject to a Gross Exposure limit of 200%.
• The Scenario: The solver pushes positions to the absolute limit of the Gross Exposure con-
straint to maximize alpha.
• The Resulting Distribution:
– The solutions essentially live on the “skin” (boundary) of a sphere defined by the Gross
Exposure limit.
– Random noise pushes the solution along the surface of this sphere, not toward the center.
• Why Metric Concentration Fails:
– The expected value (mean) of points distributed on a sphere’s surface is inside the sphere
(lower gross exposure).
– While the mean is technically “valid” (it satisfies the limit), it is distinct from the actual
behaviour of the solver, which is always at the boundary. The metric ball overestimates
the stability of the solution by assuming mass exists in the interior, where there is none.
19
Probabilistic Operations: Safety Radius
Before developing either approach in detail, we state the central trade-off. The Safety Radius ap-
proach (this section) replaces the solver’s output distribution with a metric ball B(f(x), rϵ) centered
on the expected output. This is geometrically clean: balls compose under Minkowski sums, risk
budgets accumulate linearly, and the Frobenius law holds as a tight geometric equality. The cost
is a spherical assumption — the error ball is symmetric, so non-spherical distributions (bimodal
solvers, banana-shaped tax constraints) are either missed or conservatively over-approximated.
The Highest Density Region (HDR) approach (Section 20) replaces the ball with the smallest
high-probability region of the actual distribution. This adapts to the distribution’s shape, but at a
price: composition requires a Convolution Stability Condition (Proposition 20.8), and the Frobenius
law degrades from an equality to a one-way inclusion. The difference between the two sides represents
tail risk leakage — portfolios that pass a chance constraint despite carrying residual tail risk. For
multi-stage pipelines, this asymmetry is the reason to prefer robust constraints (the Safety Radius
or Geometric Frobenius) at intermediate stages and to reserve the HDR for terminal diagnostics.
Section 20.2 develops this comparison in detail after both approaches have been presented.

19.2
Composition and Accumulation of Risk
To analyse multi-stage pipelines (e.g., Hub →Model →Spoke), we must determine how the safety
radius accumulates across compositions. The choice of accumulation function determines the logical
relationship between the step-by-step audit and the end-to-end audit.

rseq = LrP + rQ

thogonal Gaussian noise from randomized smoothing). In such cases, the Linear budget is overly
conservative. We therefore define a Composite Safety Radius consistent with the independence
of the error terms.

Compositionality: Quadratic Composition (Independent Errors)
Adopting the Quadratic definition allows us to derive tighter bounds for the composite system. This
yields the structurally important property that sequential verification is a conservative proxy for
end-to-end verification.
These results require an additional structural condition on the downstream center map fQ,
ensuring that it does not collapse directions in the output space.
Definition 19.7 (Metric Openness). A map fQ : Y →Z between metric spaces is ℓ-co-Lipschitz
(or metrically open with constant ℓ> 0) if for all y ∈Y and r > 0:
fQ(BY (y, r)) ⊇BZ(fQ(y), ℓr).
The co-Lipschitz constant satisfies ℓ≤L (the Lipschitz constant), with equality when fQ is a scalar
multiple of an isometry.
We define the Coverage Radius:
rcov = ℓrP + rQ
which measures the guaranteed reach of the sequential risk tube. Since ℓ≤L, we have rcov ≤rseq.
Remark 19.8 (Financial Interpretation of Metric Openness). Where the Lipschitz constant L bounds
the maximum amplification of errors (the gross leverage), the co-Lipschitz constant ℓbounds the
minimum: a unit perturbation in model space produces at least an ℓ-unit displacement in spoke
space. For a linear re-implementation fQ(y) = Ay + b, we have L = σmax(A) and ℓ= σmin(A) (the
largest and smallest singular values of A).
The condition ℓ> 0 means the solver is non-degenerate—it does not collapse any direction in
the output space. This holds for full replication (ℓ= L = 1), leveraged strategies (ℓ= L = |λ|),
and any optimizer whose Jacobian has full rank. It fails when the solver maps a high-dimensional
model space into a lower-dimensional spoke space with rank deficiency—for instance, a strategy
that ignores certain asset classes entirely, making some output directions unreachable from model
perturbations. In such degenerate cases, the sequential audit tube does not cover the full composite
ball, and the results below require modification (e.g., restricting the composite ball to the image of
fQ).

Guidance on Definition Selection
The choice between Linear and Quadratic composition de-
pends on the nature of the solver errors:
• Use Linear Composition (LrP + rQ) when: The errors are systematic or correlated (e.g.,
both stages rely on the same biased risk model), or when strict regulatory guarantees are
required against adversarial conditions. In this regime, the inclusions become equalities, and
the audit is exact but costly.
113


## Page 114


• Use Quadratic Composition (√. . .) when: The errors are driven by distinct randomized
processes (e.g., Monte Carlo noise, independent solver seeds).
This regime enables Audit
Safety: verifying each step individually is a sufficient condition for global compliance, allowing
the system to decouple intermediate checks without compromising end-to-end safety.
Final remarks on Safety Radius approach
The Safety Radius approach pays an upfront “com-
plexity tax” (calculating the Lipschitz constant L) to buy computational simplicity later (checking
d(x, y) ≤r). However, in many practical situations—in particular, customization and replication—
we can simply assume L = 1, as the following remark explains.
Remark 19.11 (Interpretation of L as Financial Leverage). In the context of the Safety Radius
approach, the Lipschitz constant L of a re-implementation map f : KHub →KSpoke admits a direct
financial interpretation as the Sensitivity or Gross Leverage of the transformation.
Recall the definition of Lipschitz continuity:
∥f(x) −f(x′)∥≤L · ∥x −x′∥
Let δ = x −x′ represent a perturbation or “error” in the hub portfolio (e.g., input noise). Let
∆= f(x) −f(x′) represent the resulting deviation in the spoke portfolio. The inequality states:
∥∆∥
∥δ∥≤L
Thus, L bounds the ratio of output deviation to input deviation. We distinguish three regimes:
1. De-risking / Dampening (L < 1): Consider a map that moves assets into Cash or performs
heavy diversification (shrinking the variance). The output is more stable than the input. The
safety radius contracts (rspoke < rhub), meaning the process naturally absorbs upstream errors.
2. Unitary / Pass-through (L ≈1): Consider a simple re-custody or long-only replication of
an index. A 1% error in the input weights results in exactly a 1% error in the output weights.
The safety radius is preserved.
3. Leveraging / Amplification (L > 1): Consider a strategy that applies a leverage factor
λ > 1 to the hub signals (e.g., a 130/30 fund derived from a standard model).
f(x) ≈λx =⇒∥λx −λx′∥= |λ|∥x −x′∥
Here, L = λ. A 1% error in the hub signal is magnified into a λ% error in the spoke. The
safety radius must expand (rspoke = λ · rhub) to maintain the same confidence level.
19.3

20
Probabilistic Operations: Highest Density Regions
The Safety Radius approach replaces a distribution with a ball. The HDR approach keeps the
distribution and asks: where does most of the mass actually land? The ϵ-Highest Density Region
of a distribution P(x, ·) is the smallest closed set containing probability mass at least 1 −ϵ. For
a distribution with a continuous density, this is a superlevel set {y : ρx(y) ≥λϵ} — the region
where the density is highest. The resulting “safety region” adapts to the distribution’s actual shape:
elongated for anisotropic solver noise, bimodal for discrete-constraint solvers, banana-shaped for
tax-constrained transitions.
The cost of this adaptability is compositional: unlike metric balls,
HDRs do not compose by simple addition, and the Frobenius law holds only as an inclusion.
20.1
Definitions
Definition 20.1 (Probabilistic Pullback). Given P : X ⇝Y and S ⊆Y × Z, the ϵ-pullback
identifies hubs that succeed with high probability:
P ∗,ϵS = {(x, z) ∈X × Z | P(x, Sz) ≥1 −ϵ}
Definition 20.2 (Probabilistic Pushforward). Given a tight Feller kernel P : X ⇝Y and a relation
R ⊆X × Z, the ϵ-pushforward propagates the safety region:
P ϵ
! R =
[
(x,z)∈R
(suppϵ(P(x)) × {z}) ⊆Y × Z
Remark 20.3 (Operational Meaning). P ϵ
! R defines the Canonical Safety Region for the spokes.
It answers: “If the hub x is valid, where will the resulting spoke portfolio land with high confidence?”
By tracking the HDR, we exclude tail events (outliers) that would otherwise expand the reachable
set to the entire space, rendering the audit meaningless.
Remark 20.4. Again, pullback and pushforward along an identity morphism are not trivial. When
the kernel P represents noise centered around the identity, the operations correspond to chance
constraints and robust regions:
• Pullback (Chance Constraint): The ϵ-pullback selects inputs that satisfy the constraint
S with probability at least 1 −ϵ:
P ∗,ϵS = {(x, z) ∈X × Z | P(x, Sz) ≥1 −ϵ}
• Pushforward (Robust Safety Region): The ϵ-pushforward is the closure of the union of
all ϵ-supports, representing the set of all "likely" outcomes generated by the valid inputs:
P ϵ
! R =
[
(x,z)∈R
(suppϵ(P(x)) × {z})
The ϵ-pullback and ϵ-pushforward operations compose coherently, so that multi-stage pipelines
(e.g., Hub →Model →Client) can be analysed step by step without losing track of accumulated
risk.
Theorem 20.5 (Composition of ϵ-Pullback). Let P : X ⇝Y and Q : Y ⇝Z be tight Feller
kernels. For any closed relation S ⊆Z × W and risk budgets δ, ϵ ≥0 such that δ + ϵ < 1:

Remark 20.7. It is instructive to reconcile the “simple” set-theoretic inclusion of the HDR approach
with the “complicated” arithmetic of the Safety Radius approach. The complication (the Lipschitz
constant L) is not absent in the HDR formulation; it is implicit in the geometry of the image set.
The Convolution Stability Condition
We have just seen that the composition of pushforwards
relies on the behaviour of the Highest Density Regions (HDRs) under convolution. To ensure that
the risk budget adds linearly (i.e., the risk of the composite process is bounded by δ+ϵ), we required
the Convolution Stability Condition. This condition asserts that the “likely” outcomes of the end-
to-end process (Q ◦P) are contained within the set of outcomes generated by chaining the “likely”
intermediate steps. It prevents the probability mass from drifting into the “tails” of the distribution
during composition, a phenomenon that would invalidate the audit trail.
While this condition does not hold for arbitrary probability measures (particularly multi-modal
ones), the following proposition identifies sufficient conditions.
Proposition 20.8 (Sufficient Conditions for Convolution Stability). The Convolution Stability
Condition holds in each of the following cases:
1. Log-Concave Additive Noise.
If P(x, ·) has a log-concave density, and Q(y, ·) acts as
additive noise—i.e., the output has the form y+ξ where ξ is independent of y with log-concave
density—then the composite kernel (Q ◦P)(x, ·) is a convolution of log-concave densities,
which is log-concave (by the Prékopa-Leindler inequality, see [56], [42], [28]). For log-concave
measures, the ϵ-HDR is a convex superlevel set, and the Minkowski sum of convex superlevel
sets contains the superlevel set of the convolution at the combined threshold. The mass bound
Pr(ξ ∈Aδ + Bϵ) ≥1 −(δ + ϵ) always holds (by a union-bound argument). However, the
stronger HDR containment HDR1−(δ+ϵ) ⊆Aδ + Bϵ requires additional concentration: it is
verified for Gaussian and sub-Gaussian noise (where Pr(ξ ∈B) ≥1 −ϵ holds for balls of
explicit radius), but does not follow from log-concavity alone. For general log-concave noise,
only the mass bound is established; HDR containment should be checked for the specific noise
model in applications (see Appendix B.5 for details).
2. Compact Support. If the kernels P(x, ·) and Q(y, ·) have compact support, the condition
holds by the sub-additivity of the support diameter: supp(Q ◦P) ⊆S
y supp(Q(y, ·)) where the
union is taken over y ∈supp(P(x, ·)), and restricting to ϵ-HDRs only tightens the inclusion.
Proof. See Appendix B.5.
In production, these conditions cover the principal use cases:
119


## Page 120


1. Gaussian-Smoothed Solvers: When solvers are smoothed via additive Gaussian noise (e.g.,
applying Gaussian input perturbation to Gurobi [35] as in Example 18.6), the resulting prob-
ability densities are log-concave, so case (1) of Proposition 20.8 applies.
2. Signal Regularization: In systems like Axioma [57], where the optimization is formulated
as continuous QP over regularized inputs, the mapping x 7→P(x, ·) avoids the bifurcation and
discontinuity characteristic of raw Mixed-Integer Programming. The stability of the density
function ρx ensures that the ϵ-HDR evolves continuously, preventing the “mass escape” that
would violate stability.
3. Bounded Errors: If the error kernels P and Q have compact support (e.g., uniform noise
within a small tracking error band), case (2) of Proposition 20.8 applies.
Remark 20.9 (Failure Mode). The condition fails if the solver is strictly multi-modal (e.g., a raw MIP
solver flipping between two distinct sectors based on numerical noise). In such cases, the convolution
could place the highest density of the composite distribution in a region that corresponds to the
“valley” between the modes of the individual steps. The failure of Convolution Stability correctly
flags this solver behaviour as audit-unsafe.
In the probabilistic setting, we trade strict categorical equivalence for one-directional safety
guarantees. Instead of proving that upstream and downstream checks are identical (=), we prove
that upstream checks are conservative ( =⇒). This ensures that the system may reject some valid
portfolios (type I error) but will never approve an invalid one (type II error), satisfying the primary
requirement of a risk management system.

Remark 20.11 (Audit Implication). This theorem guarantees that if we constrain the Safety Region
of the spoke to lie within the permissible set S, we effectively guarantee that the probability of
compliance is at least 1 −ϵ. Note that the reverse implication does not hold for HDRs: a set
Sz might contain 99% of the probability mass but exclude the specific high-density peak, failing
the containment condition suppϵ ⊆Sz. However, for risk management, the forward implication
proved above is the critical one, as it ensures that geometric checks are a conservative proxy for
probabilistic checks.

Remark 20.15 (Operational Interpretation). This formulation validates the use of “Stress Testing”
or “Robust Optimization” as a proxy for probabilistic safety. It replaces the integral check (“Is the
probability of failure < 5%?”) with a geometric check (“Do all outcomes within the 95% confidence
ellipsoid satisfy the rule?”).

In practice, this justifies the use of interval arithmetic or robust optimization upstream. If
we verify that the “worst-case likely scenario” (the boundary of the δ-HDR) satisfies the constraint,
we guarantee that the final portfolio is compliant, provided our verification budget δ is at least as
conservative as our construction budget ϵ.
Remark 20.16 (Handling atoms and discontinuous densities). The proof of Theorem 20.13 relies
on the existence of a continuous density ρx to argue via contradiction. In practical portfolio con-
struction, however, probability distributions often contain atoms (Dirac deltas), particularly at the
boundary values (e.g., a probability mass concentrated exactly at 0% weight for a specific asset).
In such cases, the definition of the ϵ-HDR (Definition 18.10) should be generalized to the smallest
closed set containing mass at least 1 −ϵ. Under this generalization, the proof of Theorem 20.13
adapts as follows: if (x, z) ∈P ∗,0S, then P(x, Sz) = 1, so the measure is entirely supported within
the closed set Sz. Consequently, any closed set of mass ≥1 −ϵ (where ϵ < 1) must be contained
within Sz, since removing any point of Sc
z costs zero mass. The safety guarantee therefore remains
robust even for distributions with atoms. Theorem 20.14 (Geometric Frobenius) likewise generalizes,
since its proof depends only on the nesting suppϵ ⊆suppδ, which holds for the generalized definition.
The composition theorems (Theorems 20.5 and 20.6), however, rely on the density-based structure
of the HDR—specifically, the Convolution Stability Condition—and do not automatically extend
to distributions with atoms.
20.2
Compositional Safety of the Safety Radius and HDR Approaches
The Asymmetry of Risk: Metric vs. Measure
There is a structural asymmetry between the two approaches regarding compositional safety, specif-
ically the Frobenius law.
• Safety Radius (Geometry): The logic is symmetric. Verifying alignment upstream guaran-
tees that the entire error ball fits within the constraints. Consequently, the order of operations
does not matter (up to the linearity of the radius), and risk budgets can be handled uniformly.
• Highest Density Region (Probability): The logic breaks down due to “tail risk leakage.”
In the deterministic theory (Theorem 9.1), Frobenius Reciprocity is an equality: f!(R ∩

The fact that this is a strict subset implies that the two workflows are not equivalent. The set
on the right (filtering after construction) is larger because it accepts any portfolio where the
probability of failure is merely small (< ϵ). The set on the left (filtering before construction)
is smaller because it conservatively rejects hubs that have any non-zero failure probability.
The difference between these two sets represents leakage: portfolios that pass the chance
constraint despite having a 5% tail risk. When composed with subsequent steps (especially
those involving leverage), this ignored tail risk does not disappear; it propagates and can
expand, invalidating the audit trail.
To close this gap and restore the tight audit trail required for the “Action Menu” K ⊙R (see
Section 17), we move from Chance Constraints to Robust Constraints. By using the Geometric
Frobenius law (Theorem 20.14), we replace the probabilistic check (“Is failure unlikely?”) with a
geometric one (“Is the entire ϵ-HDR safe?”). This treats the uncertainty set as a solid object, pre-
venting the “leakage” of tail events into downstream optimization steps. For the intermediate stages
of a multi-stage pipeline, Robust Optimization is mathematically preferred over Chance Constraints.
• A Chance Constraint asks: “Is the probability of failure less than 5%?” This allows probability
to leak, breaking the audit trail.
• A Robust Constraint asks: “Do all outcomes within the ‘95% likely’ region satisfy the rule?”
By using the latter (Theorem 20.14), we convert the probability problem back into a geometry
problem. We define a “shape” (the ϵ-HDR) that contains the bulk of the likely outcomes, and we
treat that shape as a solid object that must not touch the constraint boundaries. This ensures that
the “Action Menu” K ⊙R preserves its integrity under composition.
124


## Page 125


21
Transport-Based Safety
The Safety Radius and HDR approaches each simplify one side of the problem—the former assumes
spherical error geometry, the latter reduces success to a mass threshold. Neither speaks the language
that matters most to a portfolio manager: cost. Practical portfolio transitions involve a cost of
compliance.
• If a solver outputs a distribution P(x) that is technically non-compliant (e.g., 99% mass inside,
1% tail outside), the HDR approach might reject it entirely if the risk budget is ϵ < 0.01.
• However, if that 1% tail is only 1 basis point away from the boundary, the economic cost of
correcting it is negligible.
To capture this, we can define probabilistic operations using the Wasserstein metric (or Earth
Mover’s Distance) from Optimal Transport Theory (see [67, 13]). Notational warning: in the
Wasserstein framework, the risk budget ϵ denotes transport cost (a distance in the Wasserstein
metric), not a probability threshold as in the Safety Radius and HDR sections above. The budget
now represents the maximum expected turnover required to force the portfolio into compliance.
The informal picture: imagine the solver has produced a distribution of outcomes, and a small
fraction of them fall outside the constraint set. The HDR approach asks “how much probability
mass is outside?” The Wasserstein approach asks “how far outside is it, and what would it cost
to move it back in?” A distribution whose tail is one basis point beyond the boundary is, from
a transport perspective, nearly compliant—even if the HDR approach, with a tight risk budget,
would reject it. This reframing—from probability of failure to cost of correction—is what makes
the Wasserstein approach natural for practitioners accustomed to thinking in terms of turnover and
transaction costs.

Intuition: This measures the “distance to the nearest valid distribution.” It answers: “How much
trading (turnover) would be required, on average, to move the noisy solver output P(x) onto the
valid manifold S?”
21.1
Probabilistic Operations
Unlike the previous approaches, the Wasserstein framework lifts the vertical category from relations
on spaces (R ⊆X × Y ) to relations on probability measures. However, we can project this back to
a “Risk Profile” on the base spaces:
Definition 21.2 (Wasserstein Pullback). Given a kernel P : X ⇝Y and a constraint S ⊆Y × Z,
the ϵ-Wasserstein Pullback is:
P ∗,ϵ
W S = {(x, z) ∈X × Z | W1(P(x), PSz) ≤ϵ}
Definition 21.3 (Wasserstein Pushforward). Let P : X ⇝Y be a tight Feller kernel and R ⊆X×Z
be a closed relation. The ϵ-Wasserstein Pushforward is defined as a subset of the product of the
probability space P(Y ) and Z:
P ϵ
!W R = {(ν, z) ∈P(Y ) × Z | ∃x ∈X s.t. (x, z) ∈R and W1(P(x), ν) ≤ϵ}
Unlike the geometric approaches, the Wasserstein pushforward returns a set of distributions. An
element (ν, z) ∈P ϵ
!W R represents a realized portfolio distribution ν that is a valid implementation
of the hub requirement z, within a transport cost budget of ϵ.
Remark 21.4 (Serializing Pushforward). In a database implementation (see Appendix D), it is not
feasible to store an infinite set of probability distributions. Instead, one would store a representative
sample of distributions, each described by a finite set of parameters (e.g. mean vector and covariance
matrix).
Why the Wasserstein Pushforward Must Return Distributions
The structural divergence
between the geometric approaches (Safety Radius, HDR) and the transport approach (Wasserstein)
reflects the difference between Robust Safety and Economic Safety.
• Geometric Approaches (Robust Safety): The Safety Radius and HDR definitions (Sec-
tion 18.4) answer the question: “Is it possible for the solver to output a violation?” This is a
binary worst-case check. Consequently, the pushforward projects the stochastic kernel P(x)
down to its support (a subset of Y ):
P ϵ
! R ⊆Y × Z
We discard density information because a single point failure invalidates the set.
126


## Page 127


• Wasserstein Approach (Economic Safety): The Wasserstein definition (this section)
answers the question: “What is the expected cost to fix the violation?”
This relies on the
Kantorovich–Rubinstein integral:
W1(µ, ν) = inf

This calculation requires the full probability measure µ. Two kernels with identical geometric
supports can have vastly different transport costs.

21.2
Coherence and Composition
The Wasserstein approach has clean compositional properties, all following from the triangle in-

Remark 21.8 (Interpretation: Economic Justification for Pre-Trade Compliance). This theorem
states that if we restrict our hub portfolios to those where the “raw” solver output is within distance
δ of the rules (P ∗,δ
W S), we guarantee that the final realized portfolio can be fixed (“cured”) with a
trading cost of at most δ. This converts an abstract probability bound into a concrete budget for
the trading desk.
We call this a “safety adjunction” by analogy with the deterministic case (Theorem 7.7), where
the pushforward-pullback relationship is a genuine Galois connection. Here the implication is one-
directional: the converse fails because the hypothesis involves a uniform bound over all distributions
ν in the ϵ-pushforward, not just the kernel output P(x) itself. The forward direction suffices for
pre-trade compliance: verifying the sufficient condition guarantees downstream curability.
Note that the parameter ϵ in the pushforward is vacuous in this implication (the proof uses only
ϵ ≥0, which is always satisfied). The substantive content is the δ-cure inclusion: it is the cure
budget δ that controls the strength of the guarantee.

130


## Page 131


Remark 21.12. The Wasserstein framework’s main theorems rest on two assumptions that deserve
scrutiny: the Lipschitz condition on kernels and the computability of the W1 distance itself. The
Lipschitz condition (W1(Q(y), Q(y′)) ≤L · d(y, y′)) is what makes risk budgets compose linearly
rather than combinatorially—without it, the Lδ + ϵ bound degrades. But verifying this condition
for a given solver requires understanding the solver’s sensitivity to perturbations, which is precisely
the kind of information that commercial optimizers do not typically expose. The W1 computation,
meanwhile, is a linear program whose dimension scales with the support of the distributions involved;
for continuous distributions on high-dimensional simplices, this is approximated rather than solved.
The framework is correct; the question is whether its inputs can be obtained in practice. We suspect
they often can be bounded (upper bounds on L via perturbation analysis, approximations of W1
via sampling), and that these bounds suffice for the compositional guarantees to remain useful, if
conservative.
Remark 21.13 (Interpretation: Robust Filtering). This theorem validates the use of “Compliance
with Cure” workflows: strictly filtering inputs allows us to accept outputs that are merely “close
enough.” The set on the left represents “Filtering before optimization” (selecting hubs x that are
perfectly safe). The set on the right represents the outcome: a portfolio distribution ν that might not
be perfect (due to solver noise ϵ), but is guaranteed to be within distance ϵ of compliance. This
allows the system to accept slight deviations in the solver output, knowing they are economically
negligible and easily corrected.
21.4
Computing the Cure in Practice
The Wasserstein formulation has a computational advantage over the density-based HDR approach.
While computing the volume of a high-dimensional density slice is difficult (O(ed)), computing the
transport cost to a convex set reduces to standard optimization.
We exploit the following simplification (see [67], Chapter 6). If the transport cost is defined by
a metric d (e.g., L1 turnover) and S is a closed set, the 1-Wasserstein distance from a distribution
µ to the set of distributions supported on S simplifies to the expected distance to the set:
W1(µ, PS) = Ey∼µ[inf
z∈S d(y, z)]
(The optimal coupling projects each point to its nearest point in S; intuitively, mass already inside
S costs nothing to move. Existence of a nearest point is guaranteed by closedness, and a measurable
selection exists by standard results. When S is additionally convex, the projection is unique and
non-expansive, which simplifies computation.) This allows us to estimate the “Cure” (the expected
turnover required to fix the portfolio) using a Monte Carlo Projection workflow rather than
solving a full Optimal Transport problem.
Algorithm: The Cure Budget Estimator
To verify if a hub portfolio x satisfies the Wasser-

1. Sampling: Draw N samples {y1, . . . , yN} from the solver kernel P(x).
These represent
potential raw spoke portfolios (before compliance filtering).
2. Projection (The Cure): For each sample yi, compute the minimum turnover required to
reach the constraint set S. This is the projection problem:
ci = min

Remark 21.14 (Operationalizing the Cure). If the check fails ( ˆW1 > ϵ), the projection vectors
zi −yi provide the marginal sensitivities. The gradient of the cure cost with respect to the hub
positions ∇x ˆW1 can be fed back into the upstream construction process (via the Chain Rule and the
envelope theorem), allowing the hub manager to “steer” away from brittle constraints that induce
high downstream turnover.
The Choice of p = 2 for Execution (Market Impact)
Setting p = 1 in the Wasserstein metric focuses on the portfolio turnover required to “cure” viola-
tions. For portfolios of modest size, such as usually arise in customization settings, it is reasonable
to assume that trading costs scale linearly with turnover. This is no longer true for large portfolios,
since large trades have disproportionate market impact. This can become relevant when a replicated
“spoke” becomes very large relative to its “hub”.
While any p > 1 provides the necessary convexity to penalize large trades, setting p = 2 specifi-
cally is preferred for pre-trade execution analysis. This choice aligns the mathematical properties of
the transport cost with standard financial theory and computational infrastructure in three distinct
ways:
1. The Economic Model: Linear Price Impact
In institutional finance, standard execution
cost models (e.g. Almgren–Chriss [3], motivated by the linear price impact of Kyle [40]) assume
that the price moves linearly with the size of the trade. If ∆P ≈λ · Size, then the total cost of
execution scales quadratically:
Total Cost ≈Size × ∆P ≈λ · (Size)2
132


## Page 133


Setting p = 2 in the Wasserstein metric (W2) implies a cost function proportional to d(x, y)2, which
matches this quadratic cost structure (cf. [3]). Other values of p would imply non-standard impact
models (e.g., p = 1.5 implies a square-root impact law) that are less commonly used in general asset
classes.
2. Computational Architecture: Quadratic Programming (QP)
Modern portfolio con-
struction is built almost entirely on Mean-Variance Optimization, which is mathematically a Quadratic
Program (minimizing xT Σx); cf. [34].
• If p = 2: The transport cost is a quadratic term (d2). Incorporating this into a standard risk
model preserves the QP structure of the optimization problem. This allows the use of fast,
stable, and standard industrial solvers (e.g., Gurobi, Mosek).
• If p ̸= 2: The problem leaves the QP class. For p = 1, it reduces to a linear program; for
other values of p, it becomes a general convex optimization problem requiring specialized conic
solvers. In either case, the cost term no longer matches the quadratic structure of the risk
model, breaking compatibility with standard mean-variance optimizer inputs. See [15].
3. Risk Compatibility (Variance)
Financial risk is typically measured as Variance (the square
of the standard deviation), which is an L2 geometry. By setting p = 2, we measure “Execution Risk”
(Liquidity) in the same geometric units as “Market Risk” (Volatility). This allows for a unified utility
function where risks can be summed directly:
Total Penalty = Risk Penalty(σ2) + Trading Penalty(Impact2)
Using p ̸= 2 would result in mismatched units of sensitivity, complicating the trade-off between
tracking error and execution cost.
133


## Page 134


22
Estimation and Storage of Stochastic Kernels
In industrial practice, the re-implementation map f is rarely a closed-form function; it is typically
defined by a numerical solver (e.g., Gurobi, Axioma, Mosek) acting on a complex constraint set.
To operationalize the probabilistic framework (HSP-r), we need concrete procedures for estimating,
storing, and validating the kernels P(x).
This section details the methodologies for estimating the kernel P(x) using standard industrial
optimization tools, efficient schemas for serializing these kernels in the Evidence Ledger, and critical
failure modes where Gaussian approximations are insufficient.
22.1
Estimating the Kernel P(x)
The primary computational challenge is to approximate the probability measure µ = P(x) that
represents the solver’s output distribution under noise. We distinguish three approaches based on
the available computational budget and the nature of the solver access.
22.1.1
Randomized Smoothing (The Monte Carlo Approach)
This method is the direct numerical implementation of the Feller kernel definition. It treats the
deterministic solver as a black box and induces a distribution via input perturbation.
• Technique: Let S(x) denote the deterministic solver output for input x.
We define the
kernel by perturbing the input (e.g., the covariance matrix Σ or the alpha vector α) with
noise ϵ ∼N(0, Σnoise) and executing the solver N times. The empirical measure is given by:
P(x) ≈1
N
N
X
i=1
δS(x+ϵi)
(5)
• Implementation in Mixed-Integer Programming (MIP): For discrete solvers such as
Gurobi, simple input perturbation may be insufficient to explore the solution space due to
the discrete nature of the lattice. To properly induce the kernel, two specific strategies are
employed:
1. Objective Perturbation: We add infinitesimal random noise to the linear objective
vector c. This effectively breaks symmetries in the optimization landscape, forcing the
solver to explore flat regions of the objective function that would otherwise be pruned.
2. Randomized Tie-Breaking: By varying the internal random seed parameter (e.g.,
Gurobi Seed), we alter the tie-breaking rules within the branch-and-cut tree. This gen-
erates distinct valid leaves (optimal or near-optimal integer solutions) without modifying
the problem definition.
• Trade-off: While mathematically rigorous and capable of capturing true non-convexities
(such as tax cliffs), this method incurs a linear computational cost of O(N) relative to the
baseline optimization.
22.1.2
The Solution Pool Method (Single-Pass Approximation)
For Mixed-Integer Programming solvers, it is possible to approximate the kernel without repeated
execution by exploiting the branch-and-bound tree structure.
134


## Page 135


• Configuration: Modern solvers allow the retention of sub-optimal solutions found during the
search. For example, configuring Gurobi with PoolSolutions=N and PoolSearchMode=2
forces the solver to effectively exhaust the search tree to find the N best distinct solutions.
• Interpretation: The set of solutions {y1, . . . , yN} retrieved from the pool constitutes an
empirical support for P(x). This effectively delineates the “Action Menu” K ⊙R described
in the DOTS framework (Section 17), revealing the local geometry of the permissible region
without multiple independent runs.
22.1.3
Sensitivity Analysis (The Local Gaussian Approximation)
For purely convex optimization problems (e.g., standard Mean-Variance Optimization without car-
dinality constraints), analytical derivatives provide a computationally inexpensive approximation.
• Technique: By querying the solver’s sensitivity report (specifically the Lagrange multipliers
or dual values), we can construct a local quadratic approximation of the objective function.
• Approximation: The kernel P(x) is approximated as a Gaussian distribution N(µ, ΣH),
where the covariance ΣH is derived from the inverse Hessian of the objective function at the
optimum.
• Trade-off: This method offers O(1) overhead but is strictly invalid for non-convex problems
(MIPs), where it fails to capture the disconnected nature of the solution space.
22.2
Efficient Representation and Storage
Once the distribution P(x) is estimated, it must be serialized into the Evidence Ledger (see Ap-
pendix D). Storing N raw high-dimensional portfolio vectors is inefficient and opaque. We propose
four storage schemas, selecting the appropriate format based on the topological complexity of the
problem.
Storage Format

Table 3: Efficient serialization formats for stochastic kernels.
Recommendation: For the general implementation of the HSP-r framework, the Gaussian
Mixture Model (GMM) provides the optimal balance between compression and fidelity, as it is
capable of capturing the multi-modal distributions characteristic of discrete portfolio constraints.
22.3
Structural Failures of the Gaussian Assumption
While it is tempting to simplify P(x) to a unimodal Gaussian distribution for computational con-
venience, this assumption is structurally dangerous in financial engineering. Real-world constraints
135


## Page 136


frequently “clip,” “hollow,” or “warp” the probability mass in ways that a single Gaussian cannot
represent. The following sections detail three canonical failure modes—constraint hugging (Sec-
tion 23.2), the split peak from minimum position sizes (Section 23.1), and the banana-shaped fron-
tier from tax-efficient transitioning (Section 23.3)—each of which defeats the Gaussian assumption
in a structurally distinct way.
136


## Page 137


23
Handling Discrete Constraints
While the core theory of HS relies on convexity and continuity to guarantee properness and stability,
real-world portfolio construction is dominated by discrete constraints. These constraints—ranging
from regulatory lot sizes to tax thresholds—introduce non-convexity and discontinuity into the re-
implementation map f. This section details the specific failure modes these constraints create and
how the probabilistic HSP-r framework adapts to handle them.
23.1
The “Split Peak”: Minimum Position Sizes
A ubiquitous constraint in institutional asset management is the Minimum Position Size rule.
This rule forbids holding “dust” positions that are operationally costly to trade or service.
Theoretical Formulation
Let wi be the weight of asset i. The constraint is formally defined as a disjoint union of intervals:
wi ∈{0} ∪[L, U]
where 0 < L ≤U ≤1 (e.g., L = 2%, U = 10%)
This destroys the convexity of the permissible space K. If x = 0 (valid) and y = 2% (valid), the
convex combination 0.5x + 0.5y = 1% is strictly invalid.
The Solver Behavior (Bifurcation)
Consider a scenario where the “ideal” (unconstrained) optimal weight for an asset is w∗= 1.0%.
• A deterministic solver must make a hard choice: either round down to 0% or force up to 2%.
This decision is often unstable, flipping based on microscopic changes in the input data.
• A stochastic solver (using randomized smoothing) will produce a bimodal distribution.
With roughly 50% probability, it outputs 0%; with 50% probability, it outputs ≈2%.
Gaussian Failure and the Phantom Mean
Approximating this bimodal distribution with a single Gaussian—a common shortcut in risk models—
produces a qualitatively wrong answer.
• The Failure: The mean of the distribution is ≈1%. A Gaussian model centers the probability
mass at 1%.
• The Phantom Portfolio: This creates a “Phantom Portfolio” in the Evidence Ledger. The
risk system records that the “likely” outcome is a 1
• The Fix: The system must store this kernel using a Gaussian Mixture Model (GMM)
or a particle representation. This correctly identifies two distinct feasible regions (at 0 and 2)
separated by the forbidden interval (0, 2).
23.2
The “Hollow Shell”: Gross Exposure Limits
Strategies that optimize for factors (e.g., Value or Momentum) often naturally seek maximum
leverage. To control risk, a Gross Exposure Limit is applied.
137


## Page 138


Theoretical Formulation
Let permissible leverage be bounded by G. The constraint is the L1 ball:
X
i
|wi| ≤G
(e.g., G = 200%)
While this set is convex, the interaction with the objective function creates a topological problem
known as “Constraint Hugging.”
The Solver Behavior (Manifold Concentration)
If the alpha signal is strong, the optimizer will push the portfolio to the absolute limit of the
constraint to maximize exposure.
• The solution y∗does not lie in the interior of the feasible set; it lies on the boundary ∂K.
• Under perturbation (input noise), the solution does not move toward the center (de-leveraging);
it slides along the “skin” of the constraint surface.
• Dimensional Collapse: Mathematically, the probability mass concentrates on a lower-
dimensional manifold (a sphere of radius G).
Gaussian Failure and the Empty Interior
• The Failure: If we fit a high-dimensional Gaussian to points distributed on the surface of a
sphere, the mean vector µ will lie at the center of the sphere.
• The Implication: The center represents a portfolio with lower gross exposure than any ac-
tual realization. The Gaussian model implies that the solver occasionally de-leverages (moves
internally).
• The Risk: This underestimates the “crowding” risk. In reality, the portfolio is always maxi-
mally levered. A Gaussian risk model would underestimate the probability of hitting a margin
call because it assumes mass exists in the safer, lower-leverage interior.
• The Fix: The kernel must be represented using manifold learning techniques or simply
raw particle storage to capture the fact that the interior is “hollow.”
23.3
The “Banana”: Tax-Efficient Transitioning
Transition management involves moving a portfolio from a legacy state x to a target state z while
minimizing tracking error and keeping realized taxes below a budget T.
Theoretical Formulation
The constraints combine an ellipsoid (tracking error) with a piecewise-linear function (taxes):
(y −z)T Σ(y −z) ≤ϵ2
and
X
RealizedGain(yi) ≤T
Crucially, the tax function is zero for losses (unless harvesting is allowed) and linear for gains,
introducing a “kink” in the feasible space.
138


## Page 139


The Solver Behavior (Curvature)
The solver attempts to sell legacy assets to buy the target.
• Phase 1: It sells assets with losses or low gains.
• Phase 2 (The Wall): It hits the tax cap T. It cannot sell the high-gain legacy assets.
• Phase 3 (Substitution): To reduce tracking error further, it begins buying “proxy” as-
sets—stocks highly correlated with the target but which are not currently held (thus incurring
no tax).
• Result: As correlation structures shift, the “efficient frontier” of solutions curves around
the expensive tax barrier, forming a shape often described in Monte Carlo literature as “the
banana.”
Gaussian Failure and False Compliance
• The Failure: A Gaussian distribution is defined by ellipsoid contours, which are inherently
convex.
• The Violation: Fitting an ellipsoid to a banana shape forces the ellipsoid to cover the “hole”
inside the curve.
• The Reality: The space inside the curve corresponds to portfolios that violate the tax budget
(they represent the direct trade, not the proxy trade).
• The Fix: The Gaussian model validates high-tax portfolios as “likely outcomes.” The system
must use the Highest Density Region (HDR) approach (Section 20.1), which naturally
wraps the safety region around the non-convex obstacle, excluding the tax-violation zone.
23.4
DOTS: The Menu-Driven Alternative
While the probabilistic HSP-r framework (Section 18) manages discrete constraints by quantifying
the risk of solver instability, the Double Operadic Theory of Systems (DOTS) [44] manages
them by quantifying the choice. In the presence of non-convexities like minimum position sizes, a
single "optimal" portfolio often does not exist or is unstable. DOTS resolves this by treating the
re-implementation not as a function f : K →K′, but as an Action that generates a Menu of valid
options.
The Action Operator
Instead of forcing a solver to pick a single point y (which might be an invalid average), we define
the re-implementation as the action of the alignment relation R on the hub state K.
Definition 23.1 (The Action Menu). Let K ⊆∆n be the hub space and R ⊆∆n × ∆m be
an alignment relation (e.g., a constraint). The Action Menu, denoted K ⊙R, is the set of all
permissible spoke portfolios:
K ⊙R := {y ∈∆m | ∃x ∈K such that (x, y) ∈R}
139


## Page 140


Application to Discrete Constraints
Consider the Minimum Position Size constraint (Section 23.1) where a holding must be either
0% or ≥2%.
• Standard Optimizer: Returns 1% (invalid) or flips randomly between 0% and 2%.
• DOTS Action: The set K⊙R is explicitly disconnected. It contains two valid components:
K ⊙R = {Portfolios with wi = 0} ∪{Portfolios with wi ≥2%}
The "Action" does not make the decision; it presents the valid decision space to the Portfolio
Manager or the next stage of the algorithm.
Operadic Composition of Constraints
The power of DOTS lies in its ability to compose these menus safely. Complex constraints are built
by “wiring together” simpler constraints using the Operad of Wiring Diagrams.

Figure 23: The DOTS pipeline does not propagate a single portfolio; it propagates the set of valid

The “Design vs. Execution” Split
This framework formalizes the industrial separation of duties:
1. Design Phase (DOTS): The “Semantic Core” calculates the Action Menu K ⊙R. This is
a set-valued operation that identifies all theoretically valid portfolios (e.g., all valid integer
combinations of round lots).
2. Selection Phase (Human/Solver): A selection function s : K ⊙R →Spoke picks a specific
portfolio from the menu.
3. Execution Phase (HSP-r): The selected portfolio is traded, with execution noise modeled
by the probabilistic kernel.
By separating the Menu Generation (which handles non-convexity via sets) from the Selec-
tion (which handles preference via optimization), we avoid the traps where a solver tries to average
across two distinct valid options.

24
Liquidity-Aware Portfolio Construction
This section applies all three layers of the framework—the basic double category HS, the DOTS
menu calculus, and the Wasserstein transport model—to the problem of adding illiquid or semi-
liquid allocations to an existing portfolio. The motivating setting is an investor who holds a liquid
public-market portfolio and wishes to incorporate private assets (private equity, private credit,
real assets, infrastructure) while respecting aggregate liquidity requirements, capacity constraints,
and transaction cost budgets.
The foundational theory of the illiquidity premium [5, 6, 52, 1],
optimal portfolio rebalancing with proportional transaction costs [20, 21], and portfolio choice under
illiquidity constraints [7, 45] all inform the constructions below.
The economic context draws on the analysis in [54], which distinguishes liquidity as a property—
how costly it is to transact in an asset—from liquidity as a substance: the portion of wealth that can
be freely reallocated. That analysis identifies eight components of the private asset premium (pure
illiquidity premium, risk premium, guarantee value, information costs, transaction complexity, rent
of balance sheet, control alpha, and offsets such as lower apparent volatility [30]). The optimization
aspects draw on the mean–variance framework in [53].
24.1
Liquidity Constraints in the Basic Framework
Suppose the hub space is a public-market portfolio Kpub ⊆∆n, and the spoke universe ∆m includes
both the original liquid assets and a set of illiquid or semi-liquid vehicles (private equity funds,
private credit vehicles, interval funds, etc.). The spoke permissible space Kblend ⊆∆m incorporates
baseline feasibility constraints on the blended portfolio.
Liquidity requirements impose further structure.
We model three types of constraint, each
defining a closed subset of ∆m:
(i) Aggregate illiquidity cap.
Let I ⊆{0, . . . , m} index the non-daily-liquid assets.

(ii) Position-size and capacity constraints. For each illiquid asset i ∈I, let ci > 0 be a
capacity limit reflecting the asset’s available deal size or the investor’s maximum commitment
relative to fund size. The constraint
C := {y ∈∆m : yi ≤ci ∀i ∈I}
is again a closed convex polytope. In private markets, these bounds arise from fund subscrip-

Kliq := Kblend ∩Lα ∩C ∩Mκ,

The liquidity-aware re-implementation is then constructed via the optimization framework of
Section 11. Following that section’s notation, let (V, ∥· ∥V ) be a finite-dimensional space of quan-
titative attributes (e.g., asset class exposures), and let gpub : Kpub →V and gliq : Kliq →V be
continuous attribute maps that express each portfolio’s characteristics in V . Let u: V →R be a
continuous objective function (e.g., expected return net of fees). For a hub portfolio x ∈Kpub and
a candidate spoke portfolio y ∈Kliq, define the combined objective
Fliq(x, y) := ∥gpub(x) −gliq(y)∥p

where p > 1 controls the penalty for attribute mismatch and λ ≥0 weights the objective against
tracking. If ∥· ∥V is strictly convex (e.g., an inner-product norm), gliq is affine and injective, and
u is strictly concave, then Fliq(x, ·) is strictly convex in y and Theorem 11.6 guarantees a unique
optimizer y∗(x) for each x ∈Kpub, and the resulting map fliq(x) := y∗(x) is a continuous (hence
proper) horizontal morphism fliq : Kpub →Kliq in HS.
Remark 24.1 (Alignment with the public-market portion). In addition to re-implementing the hub,
we typically require the liquid portion of the spoke to track the hub. Let πliq : ∆m →∆n project
out the liquid weights (renormalized by 1 −P

Rtrack,ϵ := {(x, y) ∈Kpub × Kliq : ∥gpub(x) −gpub(πliq(y))∥2 ≤ϵ}
is closed and can be imposed as an alignment constraint in the usual way (Section 4). Pushforward
and pullback along the re-implementation fliq then propagate this tracking requirement through the
hub–spoke tree.
24.2
DOTS: Menus with Liquidity and Capacity Screens
The DOTS framework (Section 17) is particularly natural for illiquid allocation, for two reasons:
(a) Private asset allocations typically involve discrete, judgment-laden decisions—which fund,
which vintage, how much to commit—that produce a menu of feasible spoke portfolios rather
than a unique optimum.
(b) Liquidity and capacity constraints act as idempotent screens that narrow the menu, exactly
the role of projectors in the DOTS calculus.
Liquidity and capacity as projectors
As in Section 17, the aggregate illiquidity cap and capacity constraints define diagonal (projector)
relations:
Rliq,α := {(y, y) : y ∈Lα},
Rcap := {(y, y) : y ∈C},
Rmaint,κ := {(y, y) : y ∈Mκ}.
Each is idempotent (K ⊙R ⊙R = K ⊙R) and order-insensitive with respect to itself (Proposi-
tion 17.1(e)).
142


## Page 143


The core–satellite template with liquidity gate
The core–satellite architecture formalized by [4] extends naturally within the operadic template of
Section 17. Let
• Kcore ⊆∆n be the liquid public-market hub,
• Ksat ⊆∆p be the illiquid satellite universe (e.g., a set of private credit funds and PE vintage
pools),
• w ∈[0, 1] be the target allocation to the liquid core,
• Rmix,w be the blending relation that creates the composite portfolio y = w·ycore +(1−w)·ysat
from a core allocation ycore ∈Kcore and a satellite allocation ysat ∈Ksat.
The liquidity-aware menu is then
K⋆= (Kcore ⊗Ksat) ⊙Rmix,w ⊙Rliq,α ⊙Rcap ⊙Rmaint,κ.
The projectors narrow the menu from both ends: Rliq,α enforces the aggregate cap, Rcap enforces
position-level limits reflecting fund capacity, and Rmaint,κ enforces the ongoing cost budget. By
Proposition 17.1(a), K⋆is closed.
Market impact awareness
For assets where the investor’s position is large relative to available capacity, market impact during
implementation and ongoing rebalancing is a first-order concern [40, 3].
Let ADVi denote the
average daily volume (or, for private vehicles, the periodic subscription/redemption capacity) of
asset i. The market-impact-aware constraint
Rimpact := {(y, y) : yi · Wtotal ≤βi · ADVi ∀i ∈I}
where Wtotal is the total portfolio value and βi is a participation-rate limit, is again a diagonal
projector. It ensures that the spoke portfolio can be implemented without moving the market, and
can be applied at any point in the DOTS pipeline.
Remark 24.2 (Determinization). If the blending relation Rmix,w is combined with a strictly convex
objective (e.g., minimizing tracking error plus a regularizer), it has a unique solution for each input
and therefore determinizes to a graph of a continuous map. By Frobenius reciprocity (Theorem 17.9),
the liquidity projectors then commute with this map: screening before or after optimization yields
the same result. This is practically important because it allows pre-screening the illiquid universe
for capacity before running the optimizer.
Remark 24.3 (The private asset premium in the DOTS framework). The eight components of the
private asset premium identified in [54]—pure illiquidity premium, risk premium, guarantee value,
information costs, transaction complexity, rent of balance sheet, control alpha, and offsets (cf.
the empirical decompositions in [38, 23])—can be viewed as attributes of the satellite objects in
Ksat. The DOTS menu K⋆reflects which combinations of these components are accessible to the
investor after all screens have been applied. In particular, control alpha (component 7) is only
available to investors whose capacity constraints and governance structure permit concentrated,
illiquid positions—a condition encoded by the interplay of Rcap and Rimpact.

24.3
Wasserstein: Transaction Cost Budgets
The Wasserstein framework (Section 21) acquires a direct financial interpretation in the liquidity
setting. Recall that in the probabilistic theory (Section 18), a re-implementation is modeled as
a stochastic kernel P : Kpub ⇝Kliq that maps each hub portfolio x to a probability distribution
P(x) over spoke portfolios, capturing solver noise and execution uncertainty. The Wasserstein safety
condition W1(P(x), PS) ≤ϵ then measures the expected turnover required to move a realized portfolio
into compliance with a constraint set S. For illiquid allocations, this turnover is expensive, making
the Wasserstein budget the binding constraint in practice.
Asset-weighted transport cost
The standard Wasserstein distance [68, 26] uses the Euclidean (or ℓ1) metric on the simplex. In a
liquidity-aware setting, this should be replaced by a transaction-cost-weighted metric that reflects
the heterogeneous cost of rebalancing across assets:
dτ(y, y′) :=

where τi ≥0 is the per-unit transaction cost for asset i (incorporating half-spread, market impact,
and any redemption or subscription fees). For liquid assets, τi is small (a few basis points for large-
cap equities); for illiquid assets, τi can be large (tens or hundreds of basis points for private credit,
or effectively infinite for locked-up PE commitments).
The resulting weighted Wasserstein distance

inherits all the structural properties used in Section 21: it is a metric on P1(∆m), satisfies the
triangle inequality, and admits the Kantorovich–Rubinstein dual. The composition, adjunction,
and coherence theorems of Section 21 (Theorems 21.6–21.11) carry over with dτ replacing the
unweighted metric throughout.
Pre-trade compliance: the cost of exit
Before committing capital to illiquid allocations, a prudent investor asks: if conditions change, what
would it cost to liquidate back to a fully liquid portfolio? (The illiquidity discount analysis of [45]
poses the same question in a single-asset setting.) This is precisely the Wasserstein safety check.
Let Sliq := {y ∈∆m : yi = 0 ∀i ∈I} be the “liquid-only” face of the simplex. For a stochastic
re-implementation kernel P : Kpub ⇝Kliq, the cost of exit from hub portfolio x is
ExitCost(x) := W1,τ(P(x), PSliq),
the minimum expected transaction cost (under the weighted metric dτ) required to transport
the realized spoke distribution onto the liquid-only face. A pre-trade compliance check requires
ExitCost(x) ≤ϵexit for all x ∈Kpub. In the notation of Definition 21.1, this is the ϵexit-Wasserstein
pullback condition Kpub ⊆P ∗,ϵexit

rem 21.7) then guarantees that verifying this at the hub level is equivalent to verifying that the
pushforward of any hub constraint lands within the ϵexit-cure set of Sliq.

Maintenance budgets over multiple periods
Portfolio maintenance—rebalancing back toward target weights as illiquid positions are drawn down
or marked up—incurs ongoing transaction costs. The dynamic trading framework of [29] shows
that optimal rebalancing “aims in front of the target,” anticipating future costs; the Wasserstein
composition theorem below provides a complementary budget-based approach. Model a T-period
rebalancing process as a chain of stochastic kernels P1, . . . , PT , where each Pt represents the solver
that rebalances the portfolio at the end of period t. Let ϵt be the Wasserstein budget allotted to the
rebalancing at period t, and let Lt be the Lipschitz constant of Pt with respect to dτ (measuring how
much one period’s execution noise is amplified by the next). The Wasserstein composition theorem
(Theorem 21.6) gives a single-step lax inclusion with budget Lδ +ϵ. Iterating: the budget after two
stages is L2(L1ϵ0 + ϵ1) + ϵ2 = L2L1ϵ0 + L2ϵ1 + ϵ2; by induction, the cumulative Wasserstein budget
for the entire chain is bounded by PT

set the per-period rebalancing tolerance ϵt so that the cumulative expected maintenance cost stays
within a multi-period budget.
For semi-liquid vehicles (e.g., interval funds with quarterly redemption windows, or private credit
funds with periodic liquidity), the Lipschitz constants Lt vary with the redemption calendar: Lt
is small in periods when redemptions are available (rebalancing is relatively cheap) and large in
lock-up periods (rebalancing requires secondary-market transactions at a discount).
Remark 24.4 (Connection to the illiquidity premium). The weighted transport cost dτ endogenizes
the illiquidity premium into the compliance framework. An asset with high τi contributes dispropor-
tionately to the exit cost and maintenance budget, which constrains the feasible allocation to that
asset. In equilibrium, the investor requires a higher expected return to justify consuming a larger
share of the Wasserstein budget—precisely the pure illiquidity premium of [54]; see [6] and [20] for
the foundational equilibrium analyses. The mean–variance framework for analyzing this trade-off
is developed in [53], where the illiquidity premium is derived as the marginal cost of relaxing the
transaction cost constraint.
Remark 24.5 (Choosing the right framework layer). The three layers address distinct aspects of
liquidity-aware construction:
• HS (basic): Static structure. Defines the feasible space, ensures properness and coherent
propagation of constraints. Use for initial portfolio design and regulatory compliance verifi-
cation.
• DOTS (menus): Design-phase discretion.
Models the menu of valid illiquid allocations
before a commitment is made. Use when private asset selection involves judgment, vintage
diversification, or manager selection.
• Wasserstein (transport): Execution-phase cost. Quantifies the ongoing cost of maintain-
ing compliance and the cost of exiting positions.
Use for pre-trade analysis, maintenance
budgeting, and stress testing.

Conclusion
We have shown that the compositional structure of portfolio re-implementation—the chains of
transformations, alignment checks, and constraint propagations that constitute modern portfo-
lio management—admits a rigorous formalization using elementary category theory and point-set
topology. The key insight is architectural: the right objects are compact spaces (not open or arbi-
trary subsets of simplices), and once this is established, the required properness of morphisms follows
automatically. Everything else—adjunction, coherence, path independence—is a consequence.
1. Objects as Permissible Spaces: Objects are permissible portfolio spaces K, defined as
closed subsets of ambient simplices ∆n. This directly models baseline constraints.
2. Automatic Properness: Because K is a closed subset of a compact space ∆n, K is com-
pact.
This ensures that all horizontal morphisms f : K1 →K2 (continuous maps) are
automatically proper.
3. Coherent Framework HS: This automatic properness is the key: the pushforward operation
f! preserves closedness (Theorem 7.4). This allows for a well-defined double category HS where
all compositional laws (Adjunction, Beck–Chevalley, Frobenius) hold.
4. Construction from Optimization: We showed (Theorem 11.6) that practical, optimization-
based methods for portfolio re-implementation (e.g., fee minimization) naturally construct a
continuous map f on a closed domain K—precisely the objects and morphisms of HS.
5. Failure Analysis: We proved that relaxing the closed-object requirement (e.g., using open
interiors) breaks properness and catastrophically breaks all coherence properties (Theorem
10.3), justifying our definition.
The technical insight is that by defining objects as compact spaces (closed subsets of ∆n),
the required properness of morphisms becomes an automatic consequence, not an extra condi-
tion. This simplifies the framework while strengthening its conclusions: it provides compositional
coherence and path independence; logical duality (adjunction); and boundary stability
(properness).
The HS framework enables:
• Multi-stage portfolio construction with provable consistency.
• Modeling nonlinear customization: Natively supports modeling complex, optimization-
based tasks like “replace this active portfolio with a low-fee/tax-efficient passive basket.”
• Flexible verification: Check alignment at any stage.
No new mathematics was required. The novelty is entirely in the model—in the claim that these
particular objects and morphisms are the right ones for the domain, and in the observation that
standard results from topology and category theory, once properly instantiated, yield the coherence
properties that industrial portfolio management needs but has never had.
Remark 24.6. A reader who works through the proofs in Part III will find that even the most techni-
cally demanding arguments—the measurable selection in the Wasserstein pullback composition, the
integral splitting in the convolution stability proposition, the ϵ/2 tightness argument for composed
146


## Page 147


Feller kernels—are, once the definitions are in place, applications of standard machinery: the trian-
gle inequality, Lipschitz contraction, Kuratowski–Ryll-Nardzewski. The one genuinely non-obvious
modelling decision is the co-Lipschitz hypothesis (metric openness) in the safety radius composi-
tion theorems, where the insight is that Lipschitz continuity alone governs error amplification but
cannot guarantee coverage. This is as it should be. If the theorems were not more or less inevitable
consequences of the definitions, the definitions would be wrong. The difficulty in this paper—such
as it is—lives in the architecture: in the choice of objects, morphisms, and 2-cells that make the
standard results say what the application needs them to say.
The extensions developed in Parts II and III address the gaps between the basic theory and
industrial practice: a menu-driven theory (DOTS, Section 17) for portfolio customization with
discretionary overlays; a probabilistic theory (HSP-r, Section 18) for real-time replication at
scale; and a liquidity-aware framework (Section 24) integrating all three layers.
Critical Perspectives and Limitations
The gap between a mathematical framework and an industrial implementation is real. It would be
dishonest to present the theorems above without being explicit about where the difficulties lie.
Objections from Applied Category Theory
1. Thinness Limits Expressivity: The restriction to a thin double category (where 2-cells
are unique inclusions) discards quantitative metadata. Treating alignment as a binary state
cannot natively model degrees of compliance (e.g., tracking error magnitude). Capturing this
requires the richer Span model (Section 15), which introduces the complexity of bicategorical
coherence isomorphisms.
2. Loss of Companions in Probabilistic Settings: In the stochastic extension (HSP-r), the
construction of companions fails because the mapping from horizontal morphisms to vertical
relations is not a strict functor (due to lax composition). This prevents treating functions as
“perfect” relations and forces the system to treat f! and f∗as primitive predicate transformers.
3. Restrictiveness of Strict Beck–Chevalley: The strict path independence condition re-
quires the commuting square to be pointwise cartesian (Definition 8.2). This implies sur-
jectivity on points (no missing pre-images), a condition often violated in lossy aggregation
pipelines. Consequently, the “Lax” condition (Audit Safety) is the only guarantee available in
general settings.
Objections from Quantitative Finance
1. Computational Intractability of Exact Pushforwards: While f!R is theoretically closed,
calculating its explicit boundary requires quantifier elimination, which is intractable in high
dimensions (N ≈500). Practical implementations must rely on the Monte Carlo approxima-
tions or convex feasibility checks described in Section 13.
2. Organizational Barriers to Commutativity: The mathematical requirement for commut-
ing squares (Bellman consistency) implies a unified utility function across the firm. In reality,
different desks—Tax versus Model, say—operate with conflicting mandates, and the order of
operations is often an irreducible business decision rather than a mathematical triviality. The
framework can diagnose the non-commutativity; it cannot resolve the organizational politics
that produce it.
147


## Page 148


3. Gaussian Failures on Structural Constraints: As detailed in Section 18, approximating
solver outputs with Gaussian distributions for storage efficiency fails in structurally important
scenarios like “constraint hugging” (hollow shells) or “split peaks” (minimum position sizes).
The framework requires particle representations or Gaussian Mixture Models to remain valid.
4. Discrete Constraints Break Continuity: The topological foundation relies on the conti-
nuity of f. Real-world constraints involving discrete logic (e.g., lot sizes or fixed trade costs)
challenge this assumption. While smoothed estimators (Example 18.6) restore continuity in
the limit, the underlying discrete cliffs remain a source of residual operational risk.
Future Directions: The Dual Calculus of Alignment
The adjunction f! ⊣f∗established in Theorem 7.7 points toward a deeper structural insight: the
logical duality between achievability and compliance. This relationship is formally captured by the
theory of Chu spaces [55].
In the HS framework, we have prioritized the topological perspective. We viewed the permis-
sible space K as the primary object and treated re-implementation f : K1 →K2 as a forward
transformation between asset universes.
A Chu-theoretic perspective invites the dual view: treating the system of “alignment relations”
as the primary object. Instead of asking how a portfolio moves through the pipeline, we ask how
the conditions of alignment transform under the operators f! and f∗.
• The Primal View (Forward Propagation): We push an alignment relation forward via
f! to determine the set of permissible downstream relations (i.e., characterizing exactly which
spoke portfolios are realizable from aligned hubs).
• The Dual View (Backward Verification): We pull an alignment relation backward via
f∗to determine the set of required upstream relations (i.e., characterizing exactly which hub
portfolios will satisfy the downstream constraints).
This mathematical duality mirrors—and, we suspect, partly explains—the practical separation
of duties in institutional finance.
Portfolio construction teams operate in the primal domain,
designing the maps f to transform assets; compliance and risk teams operate in the dual domain,
defining the alignment relations S that portfolios must respect. The two groups reason differently not
because of organizational accident but because they inhabit genuinely dual mathematical structures.
The tension between “what can be built” and “what must be satisfied” is not a defect of institutional
design; it is the structural content of fiduciary obligation.
In this dual calculus, the re-implementation process functions as a predicate transformer: it
allows downstream rules S applicable to spokes (defined by compliance) to be rigorously translated
into upstream constraints f ∗S on the hub (executable by portfolio managers). This ensures that
the logical requirements of the destination can be automatically and correctly imposed on the hub.
While the adjunction f! ⊣f∗in the basic theory already describes this situation adequately,
it is possible that recasting the theory in the language of Chu spaces may enable a smoother
formalization of the interaction between portfolio construction and compliance verification. The
rigorous development of this idea must be left to future work.
Future Directions: Synthetic Probability and Support Functors
While this work grounds the probabilistic Hub-and-Spoke framework (HSP-r) in the explicit analysis
of Feller kernels on Polish spaces (Section 18), recent developments in categorical probability suggest
148


## Page 149


a natural generalization. The theory of Markov categories, as developed by Fritz and others [25],
treats probability distributions and kernels purely as morphisms in a symmetric monoidal category,
abstracting away the underlying measure-theoretic details.
Future work could explore replacing the analytic definitions of Section 18 with this synthetic ap-
proach. Specifically, the operation of deriving a “Safety Region” or “Action Menu” from a stochastic
solver can be formalized as a Support Functor
Suppϵ : Stoch →Rel
that maps the category of stochastic kernels to the category of relations. This formalism would
allow the coherence results for risk budgets (Theorems 20.5 and 20.6) to be derived as natural
transformations, decoupling the audit logic from the specific topology of the asset space.
Note that this perspective offers a structural definition of “determinism” essential for audit.
Rather than checking numerical variance, we may define a re-implementation morphism f as deter-
ministic if it commutes with the copy map (comultiplication) ∆, i.e. ∆◦f = (f ⊗f) ◦∆.
Even remaining within the realm of Feller kernels on Polish spaces, a synthetic framework
may shed conceptual light on an awkward aspect of Section 18: there are multiple definitions of
“pullback” and “pushforward”, each of which only satisfies functoriality and adjointness in a lax
sense (see Section 14.5 and Appendix A.3). It may also enable us to interpret the “Wasserstein
pushforward” (in Section 21) in terms of the Giry monad (see [31]).
It is of course not possible to eliminate the engineering details entirely—the topology of the
asset space, the regularity of the kernel, the choice of distance metric all matter. But the synthetic
approach provides a way to encapsulate them within the definition of the support functor, enabling
modular design of investment processes: the solver can be swapped without rewriting the audit
logic.
