## Page 157


A
Categorical Prerequisites
This appendix provides the minimal category-theoretic background required to interpret the Hub-
and-Spoke (HS) framework presented in Part I. We restrict our attention to the specific structures
used in the main text: categories, functors, adjunctions and thin double categories (also known as
framed bicategories or equipments).
A.1
Categories and Functors
Definition A.1 (Category). A category C consists of:
1. A collection of objects, denoted A, B, C, . . .
2. For every pair of objects A, B, a set of morphisms (or arrows) HomC(A, B), denoted f :
A →B.
3. A composition operation ◦: for f : A →B and g : B →C, there exists a composite
g ◦f : A →C.
Composition must be associative, and every object A must possess an identity morphism idA such
that f ◦idA = f and idB ◦f = f.
Definition A.2 (Functor). A functor F : C →D is a structure-preserving map between categories.
It assigns to every object A ∈C an object F(A) ∈D, and to every morphism f : A →B a morphism
F(f) : F(A) →F(B), such that:
• F(idA) = idF(A)
• F(g ◦f) = F(g) ◦F(f)
An adjunction is widely considered the most important concept in category theory. It describes
a specific relationship between two functors that act as “best approximations” or optimal solutions
to each other’s problems across different mathematical contexts.
Definition A.3 (Adjunction). Let C and D be categories. Two functors L : C →D (the Left
Adjoint) and R : D →C (the Right Adjoint) form an adjunction, denoted L ⊣R, if there is a
natural isomorphism between their sets of morphisms (Hom-sets):
HomD(L(X), Y ) ∼= HomC(X, R(Y ))
for all objects X in C and Y in D.
Interpretation: This isomorphism asserts a one-to-one correspondence between:
• Morphisms from the approximation L(X) to the spoke Y in category D.
• Morphisms from the hub X to the approximation R(Y ) in category C.
Special Case: Galois Connections In the specific context of a thin double category (where
categories are posets and morphisms are inequalities ≤), an adjunction simplifies to a Galois
Connection. The isomorphism condition becomes a pair of logical implications:
L(x) ≤y ⇐⇒x ≤R(y)
This is the form used throughout the HS framework to relate alignment checks in different portfolio
spaces.

A.2
Double Categories
A double category generalizes a category by allowing two distinct types of morphisms [9, 32]. This is
essential for distinguishing between functional transformations (re-implementations) and relational
constraints (alignments).
Definition A.4 (Double Category). A double category D consists of:
1. Objects: A, B, C, . . .
2. Horizontal Morphisms: f : A →B (forming a category Dh).
3. Vertical Morphisms: R : A ↛B (forming a category Dv, often with weaker associativity).
4. 2-Cells: Squares of the form:
A
B
C
D
f
R
S
g
occupied by a 2-morphism α.
The complexity of double categories is drastically reduced in our context because relations are
either true or false.
Definition A.5 (Thin Double Category). A double category is thin (or locally posetal) if, for any
boundary of horizontal and vertical morphisms, there is at most one 2-cell filling the square. This
structure is closely related to the concept of a framed bicategory or equipment [62].
A.3
Lax Functors and Ordered Structures
The probabilistic extensions in Part III (specifically Section B.3) require a relaxation of standard
category theory. When “equality” is replaced by “approximation” or “inclusion,” functors become
lax.
Definition A.6 (Ordered Category). An ordered category (or 2-poset) is a category C where
each hom-set Hom(A, B) is a partially ordered set (P, ≤), and composition is order-preserving. In
our context, the vertical morphisms form ordered categories where the order represents inclusion of
relations or risk profiles.
158


## Page 159


Definition A.7 (Lax Functor). Let C be a standard category and D be an ordered category. A
Lax Functor1 F : C →D assigns objects and morphisms such that:
1. F(idA) ≤idF(A) (Sub-unitality)
2. F(g ◦f) ≤F(g) ◦F(f) (Sub-compositionality)
Relevance to Portfolio Construction: In the HSP-r model, the mapping that takes a re-
implementation map f to its pushforward operator f! is a Lax Functor.
(Q ◦P)ϵ
! ≤Qϵ
! ◦P ϵ
!
This inequality captures the conservatism of sequential auditing. The risk envelope of the
end-to-end composite process (Q ◦P) is contained in the risk envelope obtained by composing the
per-stage envelopes (the RHS). Equivalently, the sequential audit (checking each stage separately)
is more conservative than the composite audit: passing the sequential check implies passing the
composite check, but not conversely. This is precisely the safety guarantee we need—the sequential
audit may reject valid portfolios (type I error) but never approves invalid ones (type II error).
1Our convention follows [62]: a lax functor satisfies F(g ◦f) ≤F(g) ◦F(f). Some authors call this oplax; the
choice depends on the direction of the ordering. In our setting, where ≤denotes set-theoretic inclusion of relations
(or containment of risk envelopes), the “lax” label matches the interpretation that the composite pushforward is
contained in the sequential one.

## Page 160


B
Topology, Set-valued Analysis and Probability
This appendix summarizes the background in general topology, set-valued analysis, and measure
theory required to establish the well-definedness of the HS objects and the convergence properties
of the HSP-r (stochastic) extension.
B.1
Topology and Proper Maps
Definition B.1 (Compactness). A topological space X is compact if every open cover of X admits
a finite subcover. By the Heine–Borel theorem, a subset of Euclidean space Rn is compact if and
only if it is closed and bounded.
Definition B.2 (Proper Map). A continuous map f : X →Y is proper if the preimage f −1(K)
of every compact subset K ⊆Y is compact in X.
Theorem B.3 (Closed Map Theorem for Proper Maps). If f : X →Y is a proper map between
locally compact Hausdorff spaces, then f is a closed map: the image of any closed set in X is
closed in Y .
B.2
Set-Valued Analysis and Optimization
The study of correspondences and their continuity properties is central to optimization theory [8, 58].
Definition B.4 (Correspondence). A correspondence (or multifunction) Φ : X ⇒Y assigns to
every x ∈X a subset Φ(x) ⊆Y .
Definition B.5 (Upper Hemicontinuity). A correspondence Φ is upper hemicontinuous (UHC)
at x0 if, for every open set V containing Φ(x0), there exists a neighborhood U of x0 such that
Φ(x) ⊆V for all x ∈U.
Theorem B.6 (Berge’s Maximum Theorem). Let X and Y be topological spaces, f : X × Y →R
a continuous objective function, and C : X ⇒Y a correspondence of feasible sets that is continuous
(both upper and lower hemicontinuous) with compact values.
Define the value function v(x) =
miny∈C(x) f(x, y) and the solution correspondence M(x) = {y ∈C(x) | f(x, y) = v(x)}. Then:
1. The value function v is continuous.
2. The solution correspondence M is upper hemicontinuous and compact-valued.
See [10] or [2] for detailed proofs. For variable constraint sets, the analogous result requires the cor-
respondence x 7→C(x) to be continuous (upper and lower hemicontinuous); see Michael’s Selection
Theorem (Theorem 11.10) for the case where only a continuous selection, rather than continuity of
the full solution set, is needed.
B.3
Polish Spaces and Stochastic Kernels
For the probabilistic extensions of the framework, we rely on the properties of Polish spaces [12, 14,
37].
Definition B.7 (Polish Space). A Polish space is a separable, completely metrizable topological
space. Examples include Rn, separable Banach spaces, and the space of probability measures on a
Polish space equipped with the weak topology [2].

Definition B.8 (Markov Kernel). A Markov kernel (or probability kernel) from X to Y is a
function P : X × B(Y ) →[0, 1] such that:
1. For every fixed x ∈X, the map A 7→P(x, A) is a probability measure on Y .
2. For every fixed measurable set A, the function x 7→P(x, A) is measurable.
Definition B.9 (Feller Property). A Markov kernel P is Feller (or continuous) if, for every bounded
continuous function g : Y →R, the function x 7→
R
Y g(y)P(x, dy) is continuous on X.
Theorem B.10 (Portmanteau Theorem). Let Y be a Polish space and let µn, µ be Borel probability
measures on Y . The following conditions are equivalent to the weak convergence µn ⇒µ:
1. limn→∞
R
Y f dµn =
R
Y f dµ for all bounded continuous functions f : Y →R.
2. lim supn→∞µn(F) ≤µ(F) for all closed sets F ⊆Y .
3. lim infn→∞µn(G) ≥µ(G) for all open sets G ⊆Y .
Corollary B.11 (Upper Semi-Continuity of Mass on Closed Sets). Let P : X ⇝Y be a Feller
kernel. For any closed set F ⊆Y , the function ϕ(x) = P(x, F) is upper semi-continuous on X.
Proof. Since P is Feller, if xn →x in X, then P(xn, ·) ⇒P(x, ·) weakly. By condition (2) of
the Portmanteau Theorem, lim supn→∞P(xn, F) ≤P(x, F). This is the definition of upper semi-
continuity. This property guarantees that the stochastic pullback of a closed relation is closed.
Definition B.12 (Tight Feller Kernel). A Feller kernel P : X × B(Y ) →[0, 1] is said to be tight if
for every compact subset C ⊆X and every ϵ > 0, there exists a compact subset Pϵ,C ⊆Y such that
P(x, Pϵ,C) ≥1 −ϵ
for all x ∈C.
161


## Page 162


This condition ensures that the kernel does not “leak mass to infinity” when the input is con-
strained to a compact region, serving as the stochastic analogue to the properness of a continuous
map. For the existence of measurable selectors in this context, see [18].
Theorem B.13 (Prokhorov’s Theorem). Let Y be a Polish space and let P(Y ) denote the space of
Borel probability measures on Y equipped with the topology of weak convergence. A subset Γ ⊆P(Y )
is relatively compact in the weak topology if and only if it is tight: see [12]. In the context of Feller
kernels, this implies that if P is a tight Feller kernel, then for any compact set C ⊆X, the set of
measures {P(x, ·) | x ∈C} is relatively compact in P(Y ).

The composition of tight Feller kernels is defined by the standard composition of probability
transition functions, known in stochastic analysis as the Chapman–Kolmogorov equation [27]. Given
a kernel P : X ⇝Y and a kernel Q : Y ⇝Z, their composite Q◦P : X ⇝Z is defined by integrating
the second kernel against the measure defined by the first:

system transitions from state x to an intermediate state y according to the distribution P(x, ·);
subsequently, it transitions from y to the final set C according to Q(y, ·). We now establish that
this operation preserves the structural properties required by our framework.
Theorem B.14 (Composition of Tight Feller Kernels). Let X, Y, Z be Polish spaces. Let P : X ⇝Y
and Q : Y ⇝Z be tight Feller kernels. Then the composite kernel Q ◦P is a tight Feller kernel.

B.4
Optimal Transport and Wasserstein Distances
The probabilistic framework in Section 21 relies on the theory of Optimal Transport (see [67]) to
quantify the cost of moving a probability distribution into a compliant state.
Definition B.15 (Wasserstein Distance). Let (X, d) be a Polish space. For p ∈[1, ∞), the p-
Wasserstein distance between two probability measures µ, ν ∈P(X) is defined as:
Wp(µ, ν) :=

inf
π∈Π(µ,ν)
Z
X×X
d(x, y)p dπ(x, y)
1/p
where Π(µ, ν) is the set of couplings (joint distributions) on X × X with marginals µ and ν.
The Wasserstein distance W1 (where p = 1) is often referred to as the Earth Mover’s Distance.
• Physical Intuition: Imagine the probability distribution µ as a pile of earth of unit mass,
and ν as a target hole of unit capacity. W1(µ, ν) represents the minimum work required to
move the earth from µ to fill ν, where Work = Mass × Distance.
• Financial Intuition (Turnover Cost): In the context of portfolio construction, if µ rep-
resents the noisy output of a stochastic solver, and ν represents a compliant portfolio distri-
bution, W1(µ, ν) represents the minimum turnover volume (in dollars or percent) required to
rebalance the portfolio into compliance. Unlike the Euclidean distance (which sums squared
errors), W1 scales linearly with the size of the trades, making it the natural metric for trans-
action costs.
Theorem B.16 (Kantorovich–Rubinstein Duality). For p = 1, the Wasserstein distance admits a
dual representation:
W1(µ, ν) = sup

This duality is critical for computation. It implies that verifying Wasserstein safety is equivalent

Figure 27: Visualizing the Wasserstein Distance (W1). It represents the minimum “work” required

Remark B.17 (Computational Note: Solver Complexity). The calculation of the Wasserstein safety
check in Section 21.4 relies on projecting Monte Carlo samples onto the valid constraint set S. The
choice of the order p in the Wasserstein metric dictates the mathematical class of this projection
problem and consequently the computational throughput.
• Case p = 1 (Linear Transaction Costs): When modeling spreads or fixed commissions,
the projection minimizes the L1 norm ∥z −y∥1. If the constraint set S is polyhedral (defined
by linear inequalities Ax ≤b), this constitutes a Linear Program (LP). Modern LP solvers
(e.g., simplex or interior point methods) offer high throughput, capable of solving tens of
thousands of projections per second for typical portfolio dimensions (N ≈500).
• Case p = 2 (Execution Risk / Market Impact): When modeling quadratic market im-
pact (following Kyle’s λ, see [40]), the projection minimizes the Euclidean distance squared
∥z −y∥2
2. This constitutes a Quadratic Program (QP). This is the preferred choice for indus-
trial implementation because it shares the exact mathematical structure of standard Mean-
Variance Optimization.
Consequently, it can use the same high-performance solvers (e.g.,
OSQP, Gurobi) already present in the portfolio construction stack, ensuring high throughput
and numerical stability.
• General Case p ̸= 1, 2: For non-standard impact models (e.g., p = 1.5 for square-root
impact), the projection requires minimizing a general Lp norm. This typically requires Second-
Order Cone Programming (SOCP) or general convex solvers. These methods incur significant
computational overhead compared to LP or QP solvers and may create bottlenecks if used
within the inner loops of a Monte Carlo simulation.
B.5
Proof of Convolution Stability (Proposition 20.8)
Proof. For (1): we require that the kernel Q(y, ·) acts as additive noise—i.e., the output distribution
has the form y+ξ where ξ is independent of y with log-concave density ρξ. (This is the natural model
for solver noise: the optimizer targets y and produces y plus a random perturbation.) Under this
164


## Page 165


assumption, writing ρY for the density of P(x, ·), the composite density is a genuine convolution:
ρZ(z) =
R
ρξ(z −y)ρY (y) dy. The Prékopa–Leindler inequality [56, 42] (see also [28] for a survey)
implies this convolution is log-concave when both ρY and ρξ are log-concave. For a log-concave
density, the α-superlevel set {z : ρ(z) ≥α} is convex.
It remains to show that suppδ+ϵ(ρQ◦P ) ⊆A + B, where A = suppδ(P(x)) and B = suppϵ(ρξ),
both convex and compact.
Step 1: Density bound outside A + B. Let λδ and λϵ denote the HDR density thresholds for ρY
and ρξ respectively, so that A = {ρY ≥λδ} and B = {ρξ ≥λϵ}. For any z /∈A + B and any y, we
have y /∈A or z −y /∈B. Splitting the convolution integral:
ρZ(z) =

where the first term uses z−y /∈B (hence ρξ(z−y) < λϵ) for y ∈A, and the second uses ρY (y) < λδ
for y /∈A.
Step 2: Mass bound. Define the “escape event” E = {z /∈A + B}. For any z ∈E and any
decomposition z = y + (z −y), we must have y /∈A or (z −y) /∈B. By the union bound:
Pr(Z ∈E) ≤Pr(Y /∈A) + Pr(ξ /∈B) ≤δ + ϵ,
where Y ∼P(x, ·) and ξ is the independent noise. Therefore A + B has probability mass at least
1 −(δ + ϵ).
Step 3: Convolution stability. From Step 1, the superlevel set C := {z : ρZ(z) ≥λδ+λϵ} ⊆A+B.
From Step 2, Pr(Z ∈A + B) ≥1 −(δ + ϵ). In particular, (A + B)c has mass at most δ + ϵ and, by
Step 1, the density is below λδ + λϵ throughout (A + B)c.
The HDR containment suppδ+ϵ(ρZ) ⊆A + B follows if Pr(C) ≥1 −(δ + ϵ) (for then the HDR
threshold satisfies λδ+ϵ ≥λδ + λϵ, giving suppδ+ϵ(ρZ) ⊆C ⊆A + B). This mass condition holds in
the principal applications:
• Gaussian noise: when ρY and ρξ are Gaussian, ρZ is Gaussian and λδ + λϵ is small relative
to the peak density, so C captures essentially all of the mass.
• Concentrated noise: when the noise variance is small relative to the HDR scale, A + B is only
slightly larger than A, and the density is near-constant inside A+B, so Pr(C) ≈Pr(A+B) ≥
1 −(δ + ϵ).
In general, the Convolution Stability Condition should be verified for the specific noise model in
use. The mass bound Pr(Z ∈A + B) ≥1 −(δ + ϵ) (Step 2) always holds and provides the weaker
guarantee that A + B is a valid (δ + ϵ)-confidence region; the stronger HDR containment requires
the additional regularity above.
For general (non-additive) kernels, the Prékopa–Leindler argument does not directly apply; one
would need the joint density (z, y) 7→ρQ(z | y)ρY (y) to be log-concave, which is a stronger condition.
For (2): the inclusion is immediate from the support containment.

C
The General DOTS Framework
This appendix outlines the general mathematical syntax of the Double Operadic Theory of Systems
(DOTS) [44, 51]. While the main text instantiates this theory using portfolio spaces and align-
ment relations, the underlying structure is a general formalism for modeling systems, states, and
constraints using the language of framed bicategories (also known as proarrow equipments) [62].
C.1
The Ambient Structure: Framed Bicategories
The DOTS framework operates within a specific type of double category that allows horizontal
morphisms (functions) to be treated as special cases of vertical morphisms (relations).
Definition C.1 (Framed Bicategory / Equipment). A framed bicategory (or equipment) is a
double category satisfying the following structural axioms:
1. Vertical Structure: The vertical morphisms form a bicategory (or a 2-category if the com-
position is strictly associative) [9].
2. Horizontal Structure: The horizontal morphisms form a category.
3. Companions and Conjoints: For every horizontal morphism f : A →B, there exist vertical
morphisms f∗: A ↛B (the companion) and f ∗: B ↛A (the conjoint) that represent f in
the vertical domain [62]. These essentially allow one to “turn” a function into a relation (its
graph) and its transpose.
In a thin equipment (like the one used in this paper), the vertical structure is locally posetal,
meaning parallel 2-cells are unique.
This simplifies the bicategorical coherence laws to simple
inclusions.
C.2
States as Generalized Elements
In general systems theory, a “state” is not just a property of an object, but a selection of valid
configurations. We formalize this using generalized elements.
Definition C.2 (State). Let I be the monoidal unit object of the horizontal category (representing
the trivial system). A state on an object A is a horizontal morphism:
σ : I →A
In the context of set-based theories, σ identifies a subset (or subspace) of A. In the context of
logic, it represents a predicate on A.
C.3
The Action Operator
The core logic of DOTS is the interaction between states (horizontal data) and constraints (vertical
data). This is formalized as an action.
Definition C.3 (The Action ⊙). Let σ : I →A be a state on A, and let R : A ↛B be a vertical
morphism (a system transformation or constraint). The action of R on σ, denoted σ⊙R, is defined
as the composite in the vertical category:
σ ⊙R := R ◦σ∗
where σ∗: I ↛A is the companion of the state σ.
166


## Page 167


The action σ ⊙R represents the image of the state σ under the relation R. It is the set of all
possible output configurations in B that are reachable from valid input configurations in A via the
system R. This is analogous to functorial data migration concepts found in database theory [64].
C.4
Monoidal Structure and Parallelism
To model systems composed of parallel subsystems, the equipment is equipped with a symmetric
monoidal structure.
Definition C.4 (Symmetric Monoidal Double Category). A double category is symmetric monoidal
if it is equipped with a tensor product functor ⊗: D × D →D and a unit object I, satisfying the
standard coherence axioms for both horizontal and vertical compositions.
This structure implies two key properties for system modeling:
• Parallel States: If σA is a state on A and σB is a state on B, then σA ⊗σB is a state on
A ⊗B.
• Parallel Actions: The action operator distributes over the tensor product:
(σA ⊗σB) ⊙(RA ⊗RB) ∼= (σA ⊙RA) ⊗(σB ⊙RB)
C.5
Operadic Composition
Finally, complex systems are built by wiring simpler systems together. This is captured formally
by the operad of wiring diagrams [43, 44].
Definition C.5 (Wiring Diagram). A wiring diagram is a morphism in the operad underlying the
symmetric monoidal category. It specifies a method for constructing a global relation Rglobal from
a tensor product of local relations R1 ⊗· · · ⊗Rk via composition and contraction of indices.
In the DOTS framework, these diagrams allow for the template-based construction of complex
system constraints from reusable components, ensuring that local actions (on subsystems) compose
coherently to form global actions.
167


## Page 168


D
Conceptual System Architecture and Workflows
This appendix translates the mathematical theory of the HS double category and its extensions into
a high-level software architecture. We describe a modular system designed to support multi-stage
portfolio construction, continuous alignment verification, and the coherent propagation of changes.
D.1
Architectural Principles
The system is designed around the separation of topology (the permissible spaces and maps) from
computation (the numerical solvers). This separation allows the system to support multiple semantic
models (as described in Part II) within a single infrastructure.
1. Categorical Schema: The database schema directly mirrors the double category structure.
It stores Objects (space definitions), Horizontal Morphisms (re-implementation configs), and
Vertical Morphisms (alignment logic) as first-class entities.
2. Solver Agnosticism: The core logic engine operates on abstract morphisms. The actual
execution (computing f(x) or checking (x, z) ∈R) is delegated to a pluggable solver layer
(supporting convex optimization, Monte Carlo, or discrete search).
3. Evidence Persistence: Following the Span model (Section 15), the system does not just
store boolean pass/fail results. It stores Evidence Objects (audit trails) that link specific hub
portfolios to spoke portfolios.
D.2
Component Diagram
Figure 28 illustrates the modular architecture. The layout groups the semantic logic centrally, with
the execution layer below and persistence to the right, ensuring a clear flow of data and evidence.

D.3
Execution Flows
The following diagrams illustrate how specific portfolio events traverse these architectural compo-
nents for a single stage of the portfolio construction (re-implementation) process. Compositionality
guarantees the validity of a multi-stage process, when each stage is executed in turn.
These workflows are simple, almost trivial, to specify. The complications come when execut-
ing them at an industrial scale—building hundreds or thousands of portfolios, often in multi-stage
processes, that are required to satisfy equally numerous alignment relations. The operational com-
plexity of ad hoc processes can grow geometrically. This is where the category-theoretic framework,
and associated system architecture, can play a valuable organizing role.

E
Proof-of-Concept Implementations
This appendix describes two lightweight software implementations that instantiate, respectively, the
deterministic DOTS framework of Section 17 with the system architecture of Appendix D, and the
probabilistic extensions of Part III. Both are Python applications with interactive browser-based
interfaces, accompanied by automated test suites.
The implementations serve three purposes: to verify numerically that the coherence laws of the
framework hold in both the deterministic and probabilistic settings; to demonstrate that the archi-
tectures described in the paper can be instantiated with modest effort; and to provide interactive
environments in which the reader can build geometric intuition for the operations involved.
E.1
Application I: The DOTS Framework
The first application consists of a mathematical engine (dots_core.py, approximately 500 lines)
and an interactive interface (dots_app.py, approximately 400 lines), with 28 automated tests. It
operates on the 2-simplex ∆2 (three assets), using lattice enumeration at step 0.01 (5,151 points)
for all set-valued computations.
Included.
The following components are implemented:
• Simplex and permissible spaces (Definition 2.2, Definition 2.4): lattice enumeration, con-
straint predicates, barycentric-to-Cartesian visualization.
• Three concrete relations (Section 17.2): the tracking relation Rtrack,ϵ, the fee-cap projector
Rfee≤τ, and the turnover relation Rturn≤κ.
• The DOTS action K ⊙R (Proposition 17.1): lattice-based computation of the menu, in-
cluding single-point fibers FR(x).
• Pullback f∗S (Definition 6.1) and pushforward f!R (Definition 6.4).
• Vertical composition of relations.
• Determinization via ℓ2-regularization (Section 17.6): given a regularization parameter α >
0, selects the minimum-norm element of each fiber.
• Coherence verification: automated checks of all five action laws (Proposition 17.1), Frobe-
nius reciprocity (Theorem 17.9), and Beck–Chevalley (Theorem 17.8).
• Appendix D components: a Morphism Registry, an Evidence Ledger, and a Categorical
Orchestrator implementing Workflow A.
The worked example
The central demonstration implements the worked example of Section 17.6 with the following pa-
rameters:
• Hub: ∆2 with constraint x1 ≤0.6.
• Spoke: ∆2 (unconstrained).
• Fee map: Fee(y) = 10 y1 + 5 y2 + 0 y3 (basis points).
• Tracking: Rtrack,ϵ with identity exposure maps (gA = gB = id).

• Fee cap: Rfee≤τ with adjustable cap τ.
With the default parameters ϵ = 0.05 and τ = 6 bps, the hub permissible space contains 4,331
lattice points (at step 0.01). The tracking menu K ⊙Rtrack,0.05 comprises 4,485 spoke-space points
(slightly larger than the hub, since spokes outside the hub constraint boundary can still track hub
points near it); intersecting with the fee-cap projector reduces this to 3,511 points. The menu visibly
shrinks toward the Asset 3 vertex, confirming the projector law (Proposition 17.1(e)).
Coherence verification
The test suite verifies the five action laws and Frobenius reciprocity numerically at step 0.05:
(a) Closedness: the menu K ⊙R is non-empty and well-defined.
(b) Unitality: K ⊙∆∆2 = K (195 lattice points on both sides).
(c) Associativity: (K ⊙R) ⊙S = K ⊙(S ◦R), verified with R = Rtrack,0.10 and S = Rturn≤0.3.
(d) Isotonicity: K ⊆K′ implies K ⊙R ⊆K′ ⊙R, verified with K′ = ∆2.
(e) Projectors: K ⊙Rfee≤6 = K ∩{y : Fee(y) ≤6}, with matching point counts.

E.2
Application II: The Probabilistic Framework
The second application implements the three probabilistic approaches of Part III: the safety radius
of Section 19, the ϵ-HDR of Definition 18.10, and the Wasserstein transport-based approach of
Section 21. It consists of a mathematical engine (prob_core.py, approximately 350 lines) and
an interactive interface (prob_app.py, approximately 400 lines), with 28 automated tests. It uses
the same 2-simplex ∆2 and lattice-based approach as Application I.

Scope
The implementation instantiates:
• Stochastic kernels on ∆2 (Definition 18.1): three kernel types—Gaussian, bimodal (“split
peak”), and curved (“banana”)—each modeled as a sample generator that projects to the
simplex.
• Safety radius (Section 19): empirical computation of rϵ as the (1 −ϵ)-quantile of distances,
metric erosion, Minkowski dilation, and composition of radii under both the linear bound
LrP + rQ and the quadratic bound
q
(LrP )2 + r2
Q.
• HDR (Definition 18.10): kernel density estimation on the simplex lattice, density threshold
computation, and ϵ-HDR set extraction.
• Wasserstein transport (Definition 21.1): per-sample L1 cure costs, mean W1 distance to
the constraint set, and budget-based compliance checks.
• Failure mode scenarios (Section 23): the split peak (Section 23.1) and the banana (Sec-
tion 23.3).
• Three-way comparison: side-by-side evaluation of all three approaches on the same sce-
nario.
• Composition: sequential kernels with linear and quadratic radius accumulation.
Not included.
The full categorical structure of HSP-r (2-cells, lax functors), persona-indexed
categories, convolution stability proofs, the hollow shell scenario, and production optimal-transport
solvers.
Stochastic kernels
Each kernel P(x, ·) is represented as a Monte Carlo sampler: given a hub point x ∈∆2, the kernel
returns N samples on ∆2 by adding noise to x and projecting back to the simplex. Three shapes
are implemented:
(a) Gaussian: isotropic noise x + σ ξ, ξ ∼N(0, I), projected to ∆2. This produces a unimodal,
approximately symmetric distribution.
(b) Bimodal: a 50/50 mixture of two Gaussians offset along the first coordinate, modeling the split
peak of Section 23.1. The center f(x) lies in the gap between the two modes.
(c) Banana: a curved distribution generated by x + (t, κt2 + η, −(t + κt2 + η)) for t ∼N(0, 3σ),
modeling the tax-aware transitioning of Section 23.3.
The three shapes are designed to illustrate, concretely, why the choice among the three compliance
approaches matters: for (a) they all agree, while for (b) and (c) the safety radius approach becomes
unnecessarily conservative.
173


## Page 174


Safety radius
The safety radius rϵ is computed empirically as the (1 −ϵ)-quantile of {∥yi −f(x)∥2 : yi ∼P(x)}.
With Gaussian noise at σ = 0.03 and ϵ = 0.05, the implementation yields r0.05 ≈0.074. For the
bimodal kernel the radius inflates to r0.05 ≈0.098, and for the banana kernel to r0.05 ≈0.124—the
ball must grow to encompass the extended tails even though the high-density regions are compact.
Metric erosion implements the inner parallel set: a constraint point z survives if every lattice
point within distance r of z is also in the constraint set. With a weight constraint x1 ≤0.4 (861
lattice points at step 0.02), the eroded set shrinks to 798 points for the Gaussian kernel and to 700
for the banana kernel. In the banana case, the center f(x) falls outside the eroded set: the safety
radius approach rejects the hub even though the high-density region of the distribution lies entirely
within the constraints.
Composition is verified by constructing sequential kernels P (Gaussian, σ = 0.025) and Q
(Gaussian, σ = 0.020) with L = 1. The individual radii are rP ≈0.060 and rQ ≈0.049. The linear
bound gives rcomp = LrP + rQ ≈0.109; the quadratic bound gives
q
(LrP )2 + r2
Q ≈0.078. The
actual composed radius (measured from the composed samples) is approximately 0.080, confirming
that the quadratic bound is tighter and that both bounds are conservative.
ϵ-HDR
Density estimation uses a Gaussian KDE on the simplex lattice (step 0.02, 1,326 points) with
bandwidth matching the kernel parameter. The density threshold λϵ is the ϵ-quantile of density
values at the sample points; the ϵ-HDR is {y : ˆρ(y) ≥λϵ}.
For the bimodal kernel, the HDR correctly identifies two disjoint clusters, one around each mode.
The ball, by contrast, covers the gap between the modes—a region of near-zero density. Shrinking
the risk budget from ϵ = 0.20 (the 20%-HDR) to ϵ = 0.05 (the 5%-HDR) reduces the HDR from 40
lattice points to 10, capturing only the peak cores. This contraction is the geometric content of the
monotonicity property of the ϵ-support.
Wasserstein transport
The Wasserstein-1 cure cost is computed as W1 =
1
N
P
i minz∈S ∥yi −z∥1: for each sample, the
nearest point in the constraint set S is found by brute-force search over the lattice. With a Gaussian
kernel at σ = 0.03 and a weight constraint x1 ≤0.5, the mean cure cost is W1 ≈0.015 with a
violation rate of approximately 3%.
The per-sample cost map reveals where violations concentrate: samples overshooting the weight
cap require the largest adjustments, while samples landing inside S have zero cure cost.
The
transport arrows—visualized in the interface as directed segments from each violating sample to its
nearest compliant point—make the “economic cost of non-compliance” interpretation of Section 21
visually tangible.
Three-way comparison
The comparison tab runs all three approaches on the same scenario with the same ϵ. The results
illuminate the structural differences:

In the banana scenario with a tight weight constraint (x1 ≤0.4), the safety radius ball extends
beyond the eroded constraint set and the hub is rejected. The HDR, which traces the actual density
contour of the curved distribution rather than imposing a symmetric ball, correctly identifies the
high-probability region as compliant. The Wasserstein approach, measuring the average cost of
curing the few boundary violations, finds the cost well within budget. This is the failure mode
described in Section 23.3: the safety radius approach is sound but overly conservative for non-
spherical distributions.
E.3
Observations
Four observations emerge from the two implementations.
First, the lattice-enumeration approach, while naïve, is sufficient to verify every algebraic identity
in the deterministic framework and to demonstrate every probabilistic concept. The discretization
error is bounded by the lattice step and does not accumulate across composed operations—a conse-
quence of the closedness guarantees that the theory provides. For production use, the lattice would
be replaced by convex optimization and optimal transport solvers, but the algebraic structure that
governs how solvers compose remains the same.
Second, the separation of concerns in Appendix D translates directly into code modularity. In
Application I, the Relation base class captures the abstract vertical morphism interface; concrete
relations are subclasses. In Application II, the StochasticKernel dataclass captures the abstract
kernel interface (Definition 18.1); concrete kernels differ only in their sampling functions. In both
cases, the compliance checks operate on the abstract interface without knowing which specific type
is in use.
Third, the probabilistic implementation makes concrete the paper’s claim that the three ap-
proaches occupy distinct points in a trade-off space. The safety radius is fastest to compute (a single
quantile) and easiest to compose (radii add), but fails on non-spherical distributions. The HDR
is shape-adaptive but requires density estimation and a bandwidth parameter. The Wasserstein
approach provides a financially interpretable cost metric but requires nearest-neighbor computation
against the constraint set. Running all three on the same scenario, as the comparison tab does,
allows the user to calibrate when the simpler approach suffices and when it does not.
Fourth, the visual interfaces make the geometric content of the theory tangible. Watching the
menu shrink as the fee cap tightens (Application I), or seeing the HDR trace a curved contour while
the safety ball extends into forbidden territory (Application II), provides immediate intuition that
complements the formal proofs. This could be particularly useful for communicating the framework
to portfolio managers who are not conversant with category theory or measure theory.
175


## Page 176


Glossary
Action (⊙) The fundamental operator in the DOTS framework that applies a relational constraint
to a state. It generates the set of all possible outputs reachable from valid inputs via the
relation. (see Proposition 17.1, Section 17, Appendix C)
Adjunction (f! ⊣f∗) A relationship between two functors (or operators) where one is the optimal
approximation of the other. In this framework, it formally links the pushforward (f!) and
pullback (f∗) operations, asserting that verifying a constraint S in the spoke space is logically
equivalent to verifying the pulled-back constraint f ∗S in the hub space. (see Theorem 7.7,
Theorem 20.10, Theorem 21.7)
Alignment Relation A vertical morphism in the double category HS: a closed subset R ⊆K1×K2
of the product of two permissible spaces, expressing a constraint or compatibility condition
between portfolios. Examples include tracking error bounds, asset class exposure limits, factor
exposure tolerances, and strategy identity requirements.
Unlike a function, a single hub
portfolio may be aligned with many spoke portfolios, and vice versa. (see Section 4, Section 5)
Beck–Chevalley Condition A commutation law relating the composition of pushforward and
pullback operations across a commuting square. The strict condition implies complete path
independence (order of operations does not matter), while the lax version serves as a “Safety
Guarantee,” ensuring upstream verification is conservative. (see Proposition 8.1, Theorem 8.4,
Theorem 17.8, Theorem 20.12)
Berge’s Maximum Theorem A foundational result in optimization theory providing conditions
under which the set of optimal solutions varies continuously with parameters.
It is used
to prove that optimizing a re-implementation problem constructs a well-defined, continuous
horizontal morphism. (see Theorem 11.6, Appendix B)
Convolution Stability A condition required for the coherent composition of probabilistic push-
forwards. It ensures that the “likely” outcomes of a composite process are contained within
the set of outcomes generated by chaining the likely intermediate steps, preventing mass from
leaking into the tails. (see Theorem 20.6)
Cost of Exit The Wasserstein distance from a realized portfolio distribution to the nearest fully
liquid portfolio. It measures the expected turnover required to liquidate all illiquid positions.
(see Section 24.3)
Correspondence (Multifunction) A mapping Φ : X ⇒Y that assigns to every input x a subset
of Y . In this framework, correspondences model feasible sets of portfolios or “menus” of options
before a specific selection is made. (see Appendix B)
DOTS (Double Operadic Theory of Systems) An extension of the framework that models
systems where a single hub portfolio maps to a “menu” of permissible spoke portfolios rather
than a single deterministic result. (see Section 17, Appendix C)
Double Category (HS) A categorical structure consisting of objects (permissible spaces), hor-
izontal morphisms (re-implementations), and vertical morphisms (alignment relations).
It
allows simultaneous modeling of functional transformations and relational constraints. (see
Section 5, Appendix A)
176


## Page 177


Feller Kernel A stochastic kernel that maps continuous bounded functions to continuous bounded
functions. In the probabilistic extension (HSP-r), horizontal morphisms are modeled as tight
Feller kernels to ensure stability under perturbation. (see Section 18.2, Appendix B.3)
Frobenius Reciprocity A logical law stating that filtering a universe of assets before optimization
yields the same valid set as optimizing first and filtering the results afterwards (f!(R ∩f∗S) =
f!R ∩S). In probabilistic settings, this becomes an inclusion that guarantees conservative
filtering. (see Theorem 9.1, Theorem 17.9, Theorem 20.13)
Highest Density Region (HDR) A method for defining the “effective support” of a probability
distribution by selecting the smallest closed set containing a specified mass (e.g., 1−ϵ) based on
probability density. It defines the “safety region” of a stochastic solver. (see Definition 18.10,
Section 20.1)
Hub / Spoke The organizing metaphor of the framework. A hub portfolio is the source of in-
vestment intent (e.g., a model portfolio defined by a CIO); a spoke portfolio is a concrete
implementation of that intent in a different vehicle, universe, or constraint environment (e.g.,
an ETF, a tax-managed SMA, a target-date fund). The mathematical structure formalizes
the translation between them and guarantees that the translation is coherent. (see Section 5,
Section 12)
Kantorovich–Rubinstein Duality A duality theorem expressing the Wasserstein distance as the
supremum of expectations over 1-Lipschitz functions. It allows the “cost of cure” to be esti-
mated efficiently using risk factors. (see Appendix B.4)
Markov Kernel A function P : X × B(Y ) →[0, 1] that acts as a stochastic mapping, assigning a
probability measure on Y to every x ∈X. (see Appendix B.3)
Michael’s Selection Theorem A result stating that a lower hemicontinuous correspondence with
closed convex values admits a continuous selection. It ensures a continuous re-implementation
map can be chosen even from a “menu” of options. (see Theorem 11.10)
Path Independence The operational consequence of the Beck–Chevalley condition: if compliance
is verified at an upstream stage, it need not be re-verified downstream. Equivalently, the order
in which re-implementations and compliance checks are performed does not affect the final
set of valid portfolios. The lax version (Audit Safety) guarantees that upstream verification is
conservative; the strict version requires a pointwise cartesian condition. (see Proposition 8.1,
Theorem 8.4, Section 12)
Permissible Space An object in the double category HS: a closed subset K ⊆∆n of a simplex,
representing the universe of portfolios that satisfy a given set of constraints. Closedness is not
a technicality but the condition that prevents phantom portfolios; compactness (automatic,
since ∆n is compact) is what makes properness free. (see Section 2)
Persona A parameter indexing a family of permissible spaces—e.g., the age cohort in a target-date
glide path, or a client segment in a demographic conditioning scheme. The persona framework
(HS/B) fibers the entire theory over a base space B of personas, so that all results (adjunction,
Beck–Chevalley, Frobenius) hold persona by persona. (see Section 16)
Phantom Portfolio A spoke portfolio that satisfies every local compliance check but has no valid
parent in the hub space. Phantoms arise when permissible spaces are not closed: a sequence
177


## Page 178


of valid portfolios converges to a limit outside the permissible set, and the optimizer, with
impeccable numerical precision, produces it. The closedness requirement on all objects in HS
exists to prevent this pathology. (see Section 2, Section 7)
Pointwise Cartesian Square A condition on a commuting square of morphisms ensuring that
for every matching pair of outputs, there exists a valid common pre-image in the domain. It
is necessary for the strict Beck–Chevalley law. (see Definition 8.2, Section 16)
Polish Space A separable, completely metrizable topological space.
These spaces provide the
necessary topological foundation for the probabilistic extension (HSP-r) to ensure measures
are well-behaved. (see Section 18, Appendix B.3)
Private Asset Premium The total excess return demanded by investors for holding private (illiq-
uid) assets, decomposed into eight components: pure illiquidity premium, risk premium, guar-
antee value, information costs, transaction complexity, rent of balance sheet, control alpha,
and offsets. (see Section 24, [54])
Portmanteau Theorem A fundamental theorem establishing equivalent conditions for the weak
convergence of probability measures. It relates solver stability to the upper semi-continuity
of safety regions. (see Theorem B.10)
Prokhorov’s Theorem A theorem characterizing the relative compactness of probability mea-
sures in terms of tightness. It underpins the definition of tight Feller kernels, ensuring mass
does not escape to infinity. (see Appendix B.3)
Proper Map A continuous map where the preimage of every compact set is compact. In HS, this
guarantees that the pushforward of a closed alignment relation remains closed, preventing
“phantom portfolios.” (see Proposition 7.3, Appendix B)
Pullback (f∗) The operation that translates a spoke-side constraint into a hub-side constraint.
Given a re-implementation f : K1 →K2 and an alignment relation S ⊆K2 × K3 on the
spoke, the pullback f ∗S = {(x, z) : (f(x), z) ∈S} is the set of hub portfolios whose images
under f satisfy S. Informally: “which hub portfolios will automatically produce compliant
spokes?” (see Section 7, Theorem 7.7)
Pushforward (f!) The operation that propagates a hub-side constraint to the spoke side. Given
a re-implementation f : K1 →K2 and an alignment relation R ⊆K1 × K3 on the hub,
the pushforward f!R = {(f(x), z) : (x, z) ∈R} is the set of spoke portfolios that arise from
aligned hub portfolios. Properness guarantees that f!R is closed whenever R is. (see Section 7,
Theorem 7.4, Theorem 7.7)
Re-implementation A horizontal morphism in the double category HS: a continuous map f :
K1 →K2 between permissible spaces, representing a deterministic process that converts a
hub portfolio into a spoke portfolio. Typical re-implementations include multi-objective opti-
mization, vehicle replacement (minimum tracking error or maximum return), and mechanical
substitution (e.g., swapping foreign securities for ADR equivalents). In the DOTS extension,
re-implementations are generalized to set-valued “menus”; in HSP-r, to stochastic kernels. (see
Section 5, Section 17, Section 18)
Safety Radius (rϵ) A scalar quantifying the maximum deviation of a stochastic solver from its
deterministic center, such that the solver output falls within a metric ball of radius rϵ with
178


## Page 179


probability at least 1 −ϵ. The Safety Radius approach models solver error as symmetric and
uniform, enabling fast geometric verification at the cost of conservatism. It is contrasted with
the HDR approach, which tracks exact density contours. (see Section 18.4, Section 19.1)
Set-Valued Analysis The mathematical study of correspondences and their analytic properties,
providing the rigorous basis for handling “menus” of portfolios and stability of constraints.
(see Appendix B)
Tightness A property of a stochastic kernel indicating that probability mass effectively remains
within compact regions. It is the stochastic analogue to the properness of deterministic maps.
(see Definition B.12, Section 18.3)
Upper Hemicontinuity (UHC) A continuity property for correspondences. It ensures that the
set of valid portfolios does not “explode” or drastically change shape with small changes in
the input. (see Appendix B, Remark 11.14)
Wasserstein Distance (W1) A metric defined as the minimum cost to transport mass from one
distribution to another.
In the Transport-Based Safety model, it quantifies the expected
turnover required to move a non-compliant portfolio into compliance. (see Section 21, Ap-
pendix B.4)
